From “Fifty-Two Shades of Pig: What the UCR Archive Really Asks Classifiers to Do”

UCR provenance audit

What do the 128 UCR datasets ask classifiers to predict, and what do their train/test splits test?

Repository · CSV · CSV guide

  • 52 of 128 fail task construction: staged or simulated demonstrations, recording attributes or source identities used as labels, or tasks redefined by the archive.
  • 41 have a leakage concern: 17 contain test series identical to training series; 24 more share a source across the split. 65 splits are Unclear, not verified clean.
  • Only 38 are real tasks on series sampled over time; 10 of them have a leakage concern. Real tasks include cases with undocumented label meaning.
How to read the verdicts
Task
Fails for toy demonstrations, or when the archive built the label instead of using a real source task. Metadata as label: a recording attribute, such as season or device. Instance identity as label: who or what produced the series. Task replacement: a different task defined by the archive. Toy source task: data staged or simulated to demonstrate a method. Undocumented label meaning is counted with real tasks.
Split
Concern: the test set shares series or a source with training. Unclear: not enough evidence to check; not a verified clean split. Separated: training and test come from different sources, as documented or by construction when each series is a distinct event or object with no known grouping above that unit. Related periods: different windows of the same ongoing process. Generated: source separation is not assessed for generated data. Verdicts depend on the assumed new case shown in each record.
Data origin
Temporal: sampled over real or simulated time. Ordered, non-temporal: indexed by wavelength, contour, frequency or temperature, or time axes joined end to end. Synthetic: indexed by a generator step.
Evidence
S source text D released data R inference I inference about use
  • Fails task construction and leakage 24
  • Fails task construction 28
  • Fails leakage 17
  • Real task, leakage unknown 53
  • Real task, no leakage found 6

ACSF1 DEVICE · Energy & appliances · 100/100 train/test · length 1460 · 10 classes real tasksource task label Unclearassumed new case: new appliances temporaltime single channelopen question

Taskreal task

Source task
real task appliance category (10 classes of household device)
Archive task
appliance category (10 classes of household device)
Label attached by
Source study source task S
Verdict
real task

The source study posed a classification target that a stakeholder outside the paper needed.

SplitUnclear

New case assumed
New appliances
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The 100/100 split matches ACS-F1's Test Protocol 1.0, which trains on the first acquisition session and tests on the second session of the same appliances; the archive does not say which protocol was used, and the series length of 1460 does not match the documented one-hour sessions. Whether the test presents a further session of familiar appliances, or appliances the model has never seen, therefore cannot be established. A concern would arise if the result were presented as performance on new appliances.

Source

ACS-F1 is a database of appliance consumption signatures built at the iCoSys institute (HES-SO Fribourg) explicitly to support appliance recognition: the project's own page states the data are "appliance consumption signatures and categories to be used for appliance recognition tasks".

Publication
Gisler, Ridi, Zufferey, Khaled, Hennebert, "Appliance consumption signature database and recognition test protocols", WoSSPA 2013 (the primary paper was not read -- IEEE Xplore is paywalled). The database description page was read: https://icosys.ch/acs-f1 -- "2 sessions of 1 hour each", "Acquisition sampling frequency: 10^-1 Hz", "about 100 home appliances divided into 10 categories". The UCR version was extracted by Schaefer & Leser (WEASEL, CIKM 2017).
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a 1460-point single-appliance consumption trace into one of 10 appliance categories, with 100 train and 100 test series.
Who would use it
Intrusive/plug-level load monitoring and smart-plug or smart-home products that must recognise what is plugged in; also a reference corpus for NILM research.
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (appliance category (10 classes of household device)); label origin: source task.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
z-normalization
Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
100/100 matches the ACS-F1 Test Protocol 1.0 (train on acquisition session 1, test on session 2 of the same appliances); the archive page does not state which protocol was used R R
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

ACS-F1 recorded six electrical quantities per appliance (real and reactive power, RMS current, frequency, RMS voltage, phase); the UCR series keeps one (power consumption), dropping reactive power and phase.

Dataset family
none from the same source task
Overall verdict
real task; leakage unknown
New case assumed
New appliances (units not metered before).
Split evidence
the 100/100 sizes match ACS-F1 Test Protocol 1.0, which trains on session 1 and tests on session 2 of the same appliance instances; the archive page does not state the protocol and the series length does not reconcile with the documented sessions, so the split rule is unverified R; a plug-level recogniser is deployed on appliances it has not metered before I.
Open questions
If the split follows Test Protocol 1.0 it separates recording sessions of the same appliance instances, not appliances; that reading is unverified.

Adiac IMAGE · Biology & ecology · 390/391 train/test · length 176 · 37 classes real tasksource task label Unclearassumed new case: new independently collected samples ordered, non-temporalcontour arc-length

Taskreal task

Source task
real task diatom taxon (37 taxa)
Archive task
diatom taxon (37 taxa)
Label attached by
Source study source task S
Verdict
real task

The source study posed a classification target that a stakeholder outside the paper needed.

SplitUnclear

New case assumed
New independently collected samples
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The cases are outlines of tiny biological specimens. We do not know whether samples grown from the same biological source, or photographed on the same microscope slide, occur in both sets. They could share growth or imaging conditions. The records do not let us check whether the test samples were collected independently.

Source

The ADIAC project (Automatic Diatom Identification And Classification, EU MAST programme, 1998-2001) set out to automate identification of diatoms (unicellular algae) from light-microscope images.

Publication
Jalba, Wilkinson & Roerdink, "Automatic Segmentation of Diatom Images for Classification", Microscopy Research and Technique 65:72-85, 2004 (read via the Groningen repository copy) - Experimental Results: "All segmentation methods described in the previous section were applied to a database of 808 diatom images comprising 37 different diatom taxa, each taxon (class in the pattern recognition sense) having at least 20 representatives". ADIAC project site (RBGE): "The overall aim of ADIAC was to develop appropriate image databases and analytical methods for the automated identification of diatoms for use in environmental monitoring, micropalaeontology and forensic research etc."
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a univariate length-176 contour series into one of 37 diatom taxa.
Who would use it
water-quality and ecological monitoring labs, diatom taxonomists, micropalaeontologists and forensic scientists
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (diatom taxon (37 taxa)); label origin: source task.

Data origin
ordered, non-temporal index: contour arc-length
Archive changes
contour-unrollingresamplingz-normalization
Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
Archive default split; who built it and by what rule is not documented R R
Multichannel source
no
Dataset family
none from the same source task
Overall verdict
real task; leakage unknown
New case assumed
New independently collected samples.
Split evidence
one outline per diatom valve; nothing documents whether valves of the same clone or slide sit on both sides R.

AllGestureWiimoteX HAR · Human motion & gesture · 300/700 train/test · length variable · 10 classes failstask replacement Unclearassumed new case: new people temporaltime single channelfamily: wiimote-gesturesopen question

Taskfails

Source task
real task User identity; gesture was a protocol factor
Archive task
Which of ten gestures
Label attached by
Curators redefined SR
Verdict
fails task replacement

The source study posed its own prediction task, which the archive did not keep as the label.

SplitUnclear

New case assumed
New people
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The data contain gestures from ten people. The archive shuffled and sampled recordings, but does not say that all recordings from a person were kept in one set. We therefore do not know whether test users are new. This matters if the intended application is to recognise gestures from people the model has never seen.

Source

The recordings come from a biometrics study, not a gesture-vocabulary study: Guna, Humar and Pogacnik built an implicit user-identification system in which a person is recognised from the way they pick up or handle a Wiimote, aimed at low-stakes login (e.g. multimedia services).

Publication
Guna, Humar, Pogacnik, "Intuitive gesture based user identification system", 35th Int. Conf. on Telecommunications and Signal Processing (TSP), IEEE 2012. Full text not read - the paper is closed access (OpenAlex reports oa_status "closed", no repository copy). Its abstract was read in full via the OpenAlex API: "We present an intuitive and very easy to use implicit gesture based identification system that is especially suited for security-wise non-critical applications, such as the user login in the multimedia services. The term 'implicit gesture' in our work refers to a natural physical hand manipulation performed by the user who takes hold of the control device and simply picks it up from its neutral motionless position - a 'pick-up' gesture... 100% recognition accuracy was achieved when using explicit identification signature gestures and over 91% recognition accuracy was achieved when using the implicit 'pick-up' gesture."
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a variable-length single-axis Wiimote acceleration series into one of ten gesture classes.
Who would use it
gesture-driven interfaces for consumer devices; the source study's own use case was implicit user identification for logging in to multimedia services
Task construction
fail task replacement

Benchmark target: Which of ten gestures. Source target or purpose: User identity; gesture was a protocol factor. Label attached by: Curators. Mechanism 'task replacement': the archive replaces the source's label with a different task, or defines classes by its own rule where the source posed none.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
channel-selection
Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
Archive page: instances 'shuffled and randomly sampled so that instances across datasets are not synchronized by dimension or subject'; 300 train / 700 test; no subject ids ship S IR
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded three-axis acceleration from a hand-held Nintendo Wii remote, one recording per gesture performance; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. Two of three accelerometer axes are dropped, and because the instances were shuffled and subsampled independently per axis, the multivariate recording cannot be reconstructed. Subject identity - the source study's actual label - is not released. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Dataset family
wiimote-gestures with AllGestureWiimoteY, AllGestureWiimoteZ, PickupGestureWiimoteZ, ShakeGestureWiimoteZ

AllGestureWiimoteX/Y/Z are the three acceleration axes of the same recordings; PickupGestureWiimoteZ and ShakeGestureWiimoteZ are the z axis of two gestures from the same 10 subjects, labelled by user

Overall verdict
fails task construction (leakage unknown)
New case assumed
New people.
Split evidence
the archive's 'shuffled and randomly sampled' sentence states its own purpose as desynchronising the X/Y/Z datasets ('so that instances across datasets are not sychronized by dimension or subject'), not as a description of the train/test policy; no subject id is released, and the 300/700 split is exactly 3x100 / 7x100 of the 10 subjects x 10 gestures x 10 repetitions, so a subject-disjoint split is numerically consistent with the released sizes R. Subject sharing is therefore not established; a gesture interface for consumer devices is used by people it was not trained on I.
Label
archive page: 'Classes are based on gestures', with the gesture names given as the 'original label' (donated by J. Guna); the gesture classes are the collectors' protocol factor, but the gesture-recognition task was posed by the archive, not by the collectors, whose target was user identity R.
Open questions
Subject sharing inferred from the source design (R) but not documented, so Unclear rather than Concern.

ArrowHead IMAGE · Shapes & object images · 36/175 train/test · length 251 · 3 classes real tasksource task label Unclearassumed new case: new physical objects ordered, non-temporalcontour arc-length

Taskreal task

Source task
real task projectile-point class (Avonlea, Clovis, Mix)
Archive task
projectile-point class (Avonlea, Clovis, Mix)
Label attached by
Source study source task S
Verdict
real task

The source study posed a classification target that a stakeholder outside the paper needed.

SplitUnclear

New case assumed
New physical objects
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
Each case is an outline of an arrowhead. The source assigned outlines to a 36/175 split at random. We cannot check whether multiple views or representations of the same object were kept together.

Source

Classification of stone projectile points (arrowheads), a standard task in archaeology/anthropology: points are assigned to traditions on the basis of shape, which in turn indexes the group that made them and the period they were in use.

Publication
Ye & Keogh, "Time Series Shapelets: A New Primitive for Data Mining", KDD 2009, Sec. 5.2 (read); the authors also reference their own extended review of the projectile-point literature.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-251 outline series as Avonlea, Clovis or Mix.
Who would use it
archaeologists / physical anthropologists cataloguing lithic assemblages
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (projectile-point class (Avonlea, Clovis, Mix)); label origin: source task.

Data origin
ordered, non-temporal index: contour arc-length
Archive changes
contour-unrollingresamplingz-normalization
Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
Archive default split = the random 36/175 train/test split of Ye & Keogh 2009, Sec. 5.2 S; whether points from one site or object fall on both sides is not documented S
Multichannel source
no
Dataset family
none from the same source task
Overall verdict
real task; leakage unknown
New case assumed
New physical objects.
Split evidence
random 36/175 split by Ye & Keogh S; whether outlines of one artefact or site fall on both sides is undocumented.

Beef SPECTRO · Spectroscopy & chemistry · 30/30 train/test · length 470 · 5 classes real tasksource task label Unclearassumed new case: new physical samples ordered, non-temporalwavelength or wavenumber

Taskreal task

Source task
real task sample type - pure beef vs beef adulterated with one of four offals (heart, tripe, kidney, liver)
Archive task
sample type - pure beef vs beef adulterated with one of four offals (heart, tripe, kidney, liver)
Label attached by
Source study source task SR
Verdict
real task

The source study posed a classification target that a stakeholder outside the paper needed.

SplitUnclear

New case assumed
New physical samples
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The data are spectral measurements of beef samples. It is not documented whether repeated measurements of one physical sample were kept in the same set. Without that information, performance on new samples cannot be distinguished from performance on further measurements of familiar samples.

Source

A food-authenticity study from the Analytical Sciences Unit of the Institute of Food Research, Norwich (Kemsley's group): detect adulteration of beef with cheaper offal.

Publication
Al-Jowder, Kemsley, Wilson, "Detection of adulteration in cooked meat products by mid-infrared spectroscopy", J. Agric. Food Chem. 50(6), 1325-1329, 2002, doi:10.1021/jf0108967. Only the abstract was read (via the Europe PMC REST API); the ACS full text is paywalled, so the mapping from class index to adulterant and the choice of cooking regime could not be checked.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a univariate length-470 mid-IR spectrum into one of five beef/offal sample types.
Who would use it
meat-authenticity testing labs, food-safety regulators, processors
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (sample type - pure beef vs beef adulterated with one of four offals (heart, tripe, kidney, liver)); label origin: source task.

Data origin

New physical samples.
Split evidence

BeetleFly IMAGE · Shapes & object images · 20/20 train/test · length 512 · 2 classes failstask replacement Unclearassumed new case: new source images ordered, non-temporalcontour arc-length family: mpeg7-shapesopen question

Taskfails

Source task
real task Shape retrieval over 70 object classes (MPEG-7 CE-Shape-1 descriptor benchmark); no applied problem
Archive task
beetle vs fly, a two-class pair carved out of the source's 70 classes
Label attached by
Curators redefined S
Verdict
fails task replacement

The source posed shape retrieval scored by class membership over its 70 object classes; the collection was assembled to compare shape descriptors and has no applied stakeholder, as for ShapesAll, Car and Plane.

SplitUnclear

New case assumed
New source images
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The cases are outlines extracted from images of beetles and flies. We do not know how the original images were assigned to training and testing. If different versions of one image occur in both sets, the model could recognise a familiar shape. Such shared images have not been verified.

Source

The images come from MPEG-7 Core Experiment CE-Shape-1 Part B, a database built to benchmark 2D shape descriptors for the MPEG-7 standard - not to solve an applied recognition problem.

Publication
Latecki, Lakamper & Eckhardt, "Shape Descriptors for Non-rigid Shapes with a Single Closed Contour", CVPR 2000 (read), which reports the MPEG-7 Core Experiment CE-Shape-1 that produced the image database; the outlines were extracted and mapped to series by Hills & Bagnall at UEA (shapelet-transform work), whose paper was not reachable in full text.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-512 outline series as beetle or fly.
Who would use it
none applied; the collection exists to evaluate shape descriptors for the MPEG-7 standard, and this two-class subset exists to test TSC algorithms
Task construction
fail task replacement

Benchmark target: beetle vs fly, a two-class pair carved out of the source's 70 classes. Source target or purpose: Shape retrieval over 70 object classes (MPEG-7 CE-Shape-1 descriptor benchmark); no applied problem. Label attached by: Curators. Mechanism 'task replacement': the archive replaces the source's label with a different task, or defines classes by its own rule where the source posed none; here the curators selected two similar classes from the source pool as a hard pair (subsetting).

Data origin
ordered, non-temporal index: contour arc-length
Archive changes
contour-unrollingresamplingz-normalization
Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
Archive default split; who built it and by what rule is not documented R R
Multichannel source
no
Dataset family
mpeg7-shapes with BirdChicken, ShapesAll

outlines drawn from the same MPEG-7 CE-Shape-1 image pool (instance sharing unverified)

Overall verdict
fails task construction (leakage unknown)
New case assumed
New source images.
Split evidence
20 distinct MPEG-7 shapes per class; nothing documents shared source images across the split R.
Label
the archive page states that the curators extracted the outlines and defined the two-class problem S; that the pair was chosen for its similarity is our reading R. The class names are the source's, the task is the curators'.
Open questions
Subsetting of a source class set: the two class names come from MPEG-7, the two-class task from the curators; the source protocol was retrieval, not classification.

Source

No real-world problem exists. BME (Begin-Middle-End) is a synthetic three-class benchmark: every series is a central plate (positive or negative) and the class is decided by whether a small positive bell appears at the start of the series, nowhere, or at the end.

Publication
No publication found. The most likely origin is the Grenoble AMA group of A. Douzal-Chouakria (candidate: Frambourg, Douzal-Chouakria & Gaussier, "Learning Multiple Temporal Matching for Time Series Classification", IDA 2013), but that full text could not be reached (hal.science/hal-00908431 returns an access-denied page, ResearchGate returns HTTP 403) and no source consulted states that BME was introduced there. Nothing about the generator (bell amplitude, width, noise model) is documented.

Publication

Why
The audit describes cases taken at nearby times in one simulated system. A training case and test case can therefore describe almost the same system conditions. The result tests more cases from that simulation. It does not show whether the model works on a new simulation run; similarity between cases would need a separate check.

Source

EPANET simulation of chlorine concentration at every node of a drinking-water network over 15 simulated days, produced by Li et al. (KDD 2009) to evaluate a missing-value imputation method; no domain classification target was ever defined for it.

Publication
Li, McCann, Pollard, Faloutsos, "DynaMMo - Mining and Summarization of Coevolving Sequences with Missing Values", KDD 2009, Sec. 4.1.2 Datasets (read) - "Chlorine Dataset (Chlorine) - The Chlorine dataset (see sample in Figure 6(a)) was produced by EPANET 2 that models the hydraulic and water quality behavior of water distribution piping systems. ... The data set consists of 166 nodes (pipe junctions) and measurement of the Chlorine concentration level at all these nodes during 15 days (one measurement for every 5 minutes, a total of 4310 time ticks). Since the water demand pattern during the 15 days follows a clear global periodic pattern (daily cycle, dominating residential demand pattern), EPANET would correctly reflect the pattern in the Chlorine concentration with a few exceptions and slight time shifts." The archive page attributes the data to Lei Li's CMU PhD thesis; both links it gives (cs.cmu.edu/~leili/pubs/leili-thesis.pdf and .../li-kdd09.pdf) are dead (HTTP 404), and the thesis itself was not reached - the KDD'09 paper above is the same data described by the same authors.

The survey's installing electricians annotated each monitored product with its appliance ("selected from a dropdown list"), so under policy L3 we count appliance-type recognition as the source's own task although the survey trained no classifier; the classification problem was posed on those annotations by the curators (Lines & Bagnall, SDM 2014; Lines 2015 thesis). Each input is an appliance trace metered on its own, so the task is appliance recognition, not whole-house disaggregation.

Why
The source measured each physical appliance over many days. We do not know whether all days from an appliance or household were kept in one set. If the same appliance appears in both sets, the model may recognise its usual pattern. That would be a narrower test than recognising appliance types in new homes.

Source

These series come from the UK government-sponsored household electricity monitoring study known as "Powering the Nation" (the DECC/Defra/Energy Saving Trust Household Electricity Survey).

Publication
UK Household Electricity Survey (DECC, Defra and the Energy Saving Trust, 2010-2011); Intertek Report R66141, "Household Electricity Survey: A study of domestic electrical product usage", final report issue 4. Household counts differ: the archive page, the thesis and Intertek (p. 24) give 251 households, Report 5 (p. 4) and gov.uk give 250; the 26 year-long households were sampled every 10 minutes except from late November 2010 to February 2011, when they recorded every 2 minutes (Intertek p. 64). Curated into TSC datasets by Lines & Bagnall (UEA): J. Lines, PhD thesis, UEA, 2015, Sec. 3.2.2 and Table 3.3 ("We provide five new classification problems that focus on domestic appliance classification from electricity consumption"); introduced in Lines & Bagnall, "Ensembles of elastic distance measures for time series classification", SIAM SDM 2014 (not read: no full text found).

Source

Automatic annotation of cricket matches from the officials' body motion: umpires signal match events (four, six, wide, no-ball, out, ...) with codified arm gestures, and the study instrumented umpires with wrist-worn accelerometers so that these signals could be recognised online and used to label the video/scorecard.

Publication
Ko, West, Venkatesh, Kumar, "Online Context Recognition in Multisensor Systems using Dynamic Time Warping", ISSNIP 2005 (DOI 10.1109/ISSNIP.2005.1595593). Not read: the paper is closed access (Unpaywall reports oa_status "closed"; the archive's own second_link is a dead IEEE Xplore login URL, and the Semantic Scholar "open access" copy at dro.deakin.edu.au returns an empty file). Layer 1 above therefore rests on the archive description page, the UCR briefing document and the UEA archive paper's description of the multivariate version.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a normalized length-300 single-axis wrist-accelerometer series as one of twelve cricket umpire signals.
Who would use it
sports-video annotation and automatic scoring systems; more generally the source authors' interest was online context recognition from wearable multisensor streams
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (which of twelve cricket umpire signals was performed); label origin: source task.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
channel-selectionz-normalization
Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
Archive default split; who built it and by what rule is not documented R R
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded acceleration from wrist-worn accelerometers; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. Five of the six original channels are dropped (both wrists x three axes reduced to one axis), so gestures that differ mainly in the other arm's motion or in the plane of movement become harder or impossible to separate. Amplitude is removed by the donors' normalization, and the length normalization to 300 removes gesture duration. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive, where it already exists as Cricket.

Dataset family
cricket with CricketY, CricketZ, CricketUEA

X, Y and Z are the same recordings projected onto three axes; UEA Cricket is the multivariate version

Overall verdict
real task; leakage unknown
New case assumed
New people.
Split evidence
390/390 split not documented as subject-disjoint; no subject id in the files R.

Source

Automatic annotation of cricket matches from the officials' body motion: umpires signal match events (four, six, wide, no-ball, out, ...) with codified arm gestures, and the study instrumented umpires with wrist-worn accelerometers so that these signals could be recognised online and used to label the video/scorecard.

Publication

land-cover / crop class of a ground pixel (24 classes)

real task

The source study posed a classification target that a stakeholder outside the paper needed.

SplitConcern

New case assumed
New fields, scenes or seasons
Exact duplicates
2,753 of 16,800 test series (16.4%) exactly match a training series D
Why
The cases are pixel sequences from one scene and season. Nearby pixels may describe the same field and growing conditions. The value check also finds 2,753 of 16,800 test cases identical to training cases. This makes the test different from evaluating new fields, scenes or seasons.

Source

Land-cover / crop-type mapping from satellite image time series. Tan, Webb & Petitjean (SDM 2017, Sec. on SITS classification) describe the source data: "In this work, we used 46 geometrically and radiometrically corrected images taken by FORMOSAT-2 satellite...

Publication
Tan, Webb & Petitjean, "Indexing and classifying gigabytes of time series under time warping", SDM 2017 (read; the archive's data_source_link bit.ly/SDM2017 resolves to the figshare project for this paper). The related TempCNN paper (Pelletier, Webb & Petitjean, arXiv:1811.10166, Sec. 3.1-3.2) describes the same imagery: "The satellite dataset is composed of 46 Formosat-2 images acquired at 8 meter spatial resolution during the year 2006", with reference data from "farmer's declaration from 2006... ground field campaigns performed during 2006... and a reference map obtained with a semi-automatic procedure" (that paper aggregates to 13 classes rather than 24).
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-46 per-pixel reflectance profile into one of 24 land-cover classes.
Who would use it
agricultural and environmental agencies producing land-cover maps (the data were released with permission of CesBIO and the French Space Agency)
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (land-cover / crop class of a ground pixel (24 classes)); label origin: source task.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
channel-selectionz-normalization
Leakage
fail exact cross-split matches (2753 of 16800 test series)
Split sources
Tan et al. (2017) release, 7200 train / 16800 test pixels of one scene and season; assignment rule undocumented R DR
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded sequence of satellite reflectance observations for one 8 m ground pixel over a growing season; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. Spatial context around the pixel is dropped (each pixel is treated independently), the multi-band signal is reduced to one channel, and z-normalization removes the absolute reflectance level, which is physically informative for land cover. The temporal axis itself is preserved intact. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Dataset family
none from the same source task
Overall verdict
fails leakage
New case assumed
New fields, scenes or seasons.
Split evidence
all pixels come from one 64 km2 scene and one season; neighbouring pixels of one field are near-duplicates and 2753 of 16800 test pixels are identical to training pixels; a land-cover model is deployed on new fields and years R+D.

DodgerLoopDay SENSOR · Traffic & pedestrians · 78/80 train/test · length 288 · 7 classes failsmetadata as label Related periodsassumed new case: new days or later periods temporaltime family: dodger-loopopen question

Taskfails

Source task
real task Unsupervised detection of baseball-game traffic events (crowds leaving Dodger Stadium)
Archive task
Day of week
Label attached by
Curators metadata SR
Verdict
fails metadata as label

The source posed unsupervised detection of traffic events caused by baseball games (UCI: predicting the presence of a game), not day-of-week prediction.

SplitRelated periods

New case assumed
New days or later periods
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
Days from one traffic detector are on both sides of the split; how they were assigned is not documented. Training and test days can be part of the same baseball home stand or share similar traffic conditions. The test contains different days, but does not specifically evaluate a later period or another detector. This relationship should be considered when interpreting the score.

Source

Ihler, Hutchins & Smyth collected freeway loop-detector counts to study *unsupervised event detection*: learning a time-varying Poisson model of normal traffic so that anomalous bursts (crowds leaving Dodger Stadium after a baseball game) can be flagged without being told when games occurred.

Publication
Ihler, Hutchins & Smyth, "Learning to Detect Events with Markov-Modulated Poisson Processes", ACM TKDD 1(3), Art. 13, 2007 (journal version of the KDD 2006 paper the archive cites; read in full), Sec. 3, p. 6: "The second dataset will be referred to as the freeway traffic data and consists of estimated vehicle counts every 5 minutes over 6 months from an inductive loop-sensor located on the Glendale on-ramp to the 101-North freeway in Los Angeles." Same page: "the baseball-related events at this on-ramp correspond to traffic leaving at the end of a game". The UCI "Dodgers Loop Sensor" page (read) confirms "Loop sensor data was collected for the Glendale on ramp for the 101 North freeway in Los Angeles", 288 five-minute observations per day over 25 weeks, and states the goal as predicting "the presence of a baseball game at Dodgers stadium".
Full record
Benchmark task
Given a 288-point 24-hour on-ramp traffic profile (with missing values), predict which day of the week it is.
Who would use it
traffic operations / event detection (Caltrans PeMS-style monitoring); for the day-of-week label specifically there is no real stakeholder, since the calendar already supplies the answer
Task construction
fail metadata as label

Benchmark target: Day of week. Source target or purpose: Unsupervised event detection; no class target. Label attached by: Curators. Mechanism 'metadata as label': the archive derives the classes from a recording attribute.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
windowing
Leakage
pass (related periods) different periods of one ongoing process
Split sources
Days of one loop detector assigned to train/test at random by the archive contributor (C. Yeh) R R
Multichannel source
no
Dataset family
dodger-loop with DodgerLoopGame, DodgerLoopWeekend

the same 158 daily windows of one loop-detector recording, labelled three ways

Overall verdict
fails task construction
New case assumed
New days or later periods.
Split evidence
158 consecutive days of one loop detector split at random; deployment on that ramp sees new days, but neighbouring days of the same home stand sit on both sides R.
Label
day-of-week label derived by the archive contributor (C. Yeh) from timestamps; Ihler et al. collected counts for event detection R. The day-of-week label was derived by the archive contributor from timestamps; the source study collected counts for unsupervised event detection R.
Open questions
Related periods: different days of one detector; not counted as leakage. Whether related periods should count as clean is undecided.

The test contains different days, but does not specifically evaluate a later period or another detector. This relationship should be considered when interpreting the score.]

Source

Same recording and same study as DodgerLoopDay: Ihler, Hutchins & Smyth modelled 5-minute vehicle counts from an inductive loop detector on the Glendale on-ramp of the 101-North freeway in Los Angeles, next to Dodger Stadium, in order to detect anomalous crowd events *without* being told when games happened.

Publication
Ihler, Hutchins & Smyth, "Learning to Detect Events with Markov-Modulated Poisson Processes", ACM TKDD 1(3), Art. 13, 2007 (journal version of the KDD 2006 paper the archive cites; read in full), Sec. 3, p. 6: "The second dataset will be referred to as the freeway traffic data and consists of estimated vehicle counts every 5 minutes over 6 months from an inductive loop-sensor located on the Glendale on-ramp to the 101-North freeway in Los Angeles." Same page, on the labels: "The lower panel of Figure 4 shows a set of known (ground-truth) events for this data (which are unknown to the model and only used for validation) corresponding to the dates and times of baseball games." And: "the baseball-related events at this on-ramp correspond to traffic leaving at the end of a game when large numbers of individuals leave the stadium and get on the freeway; thus, the event has a signature in the data that will tend to lag in time behind that of the baseball game itself."
Full record
Benchmark task
Given a 288-point 24-hour on-ramp traffic profile (with missing values), decide whether that day was a Dodgers home-game day.
Who would use it
traffic operations and event-aware routing; the underlying capability (detecting stadium egress from ramp counts) is genuinely useful, though the game schedule itself is public
Task construction
fail metadata as label

The benchmark label (home-game day) comes from the published game schedule attached to each date; the source used that schedule only as validation ground truth for unsupervised event detection; label origin: metadata.

pass (related periods) different periods of one ongoing process

no

dodger-loop with DodgerLoopDay, DodgerLoopWeekend

the same 158 daily windows of one loop-detector recording, labelled three ways

Overall verdict

New days or later periods.

158 consecutive days of one loop detector split at random; deployment on that ramp sees new days, but neighbouring days of the same home stand sit on both sides R.
Label
label from the game schedule; the UCI source problem was detecting games from loop counts, so the label is the source's own target R. The game-day label comes from the Dodgers home-game schedule attached to the date; treated as the source's own target because the source study validated its unsupervised detector against that schedule R.
Open questions
Label from the game schedule attached to the date (calendar metadata) while the sibling DodgerLoopDay/Weekend labels are listed under metadata; we count the game label as the source's own target because Ihler et al. validated their event detector against the game schedule. Source

Same recording and same study as DodgerLoopDay and DodgerLoopGame: Ihler, Hutchins & Smyth modelled 5-minute vehicle counts from an inductive loop detector on the Glendale on-ramp of the 101-North freeway in Los Angeles.

Publication
Ihler, Hutchins & Smyth, "Learning to Detect Events with Markov-Modulated Poisson Processes", ACM TKDD 1(3), Art. 13, 2007 (journal version of the KDD 2006 paper the archive cites; read in full), Sec. 3, p. 6: "The second dataset will be referred to as the freeway traffic data and consists of estimated vehicle counts every 5 minutes over 6 months from an inductive loop-sensor located on the Glendale on-ramp to the 101-North freeway in Los Angeles. Figure 4 shows the temporal pattern for a particular week starting with Sunday morning and ending Saturday night. The daily rhythms of traffic flow are clearly visible, as is the distinction between weekdays and weekends." The UCI "Dodgers Loop Sensor" page (read) confirms 288 five-minute observations per day over 25 weeks.

Earthquakes SENSOR · Earth & space science · 322/139 train/test · length 512 · 2 classes failstask replacement Related periodsassumed new case: new periods or event groups temporaltime open question

none Continuous seismic monitoring; no class target

Whether a large event follows a window; curator thresholds

Curators redefined S
Verdict
fails task replacement

The source collected the data but posed no classification target.

SplitRelated periods

New case assumed
New periods or event groups
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The time windows are described as non-overlapping, so shared time samples are not an established problem here. However, windows from one earthquake sequence can be assigned to both sets. They may share the same broader event. The split does not specifically test later periods or separately collected event groups.

Source

There is no external "original problem" here in the sense of a domain study that posed this task. The underlying data are hourly averaged seismic readings from the Northern California Earthquake Data Center (NCEDC), Dec 1967 to 2003, a monitoring archive rather than an experiment.

Publication
timeseriesclassification.com description page for Earthquakes, which is the curators' own account: "We transform this single time series into a classification problem by first defining a major event as any reading of over 5 on the Rictor scale... Hence we consider a positive case to be one where a major event is not preceded by another major event for at least 512 hours. To construct a negative case, we consider instances where there is a reading below 4... that is preceded by at least 20 readings in the previous 512 hours that are non-zero (to avoid trivial negative cases). None of the cases overlap in time (i.e. we perform a segmentation rather than use a sliding window). Of the 86,066 hourly readings, we produce 368 negative cases and 93 positive." No separate peer-reviewed paper describing this curation was located, nor documentation of exactly which NCEDC quantity was "averaged" per hour.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a 512-point hourly seismic window as preceding a major event or not.
Who would use it
seismic-hazard monitoring agencies and earthquake early-warning services, in principle; in practice the dataset was assembled as a TSC benchmark
Task construction
fail task replacement

Benchmark target: Whether a large event follows a window; curator thresholds. Source target or purpose: Continuous seismic monitoring; no class target. Label attached by: Curators. Mechanism 'task replacement': the archive replaces the source's label with a different task, or defines classes by its own rule where the source posed none.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
windowing
Leakage
pass (related periods) different periods of one ongoing process
Split sources
Curators' windows (non-overlapping 21-day windows of one 1967-2003 record) split at random by the curators R S
Multichannel source
no
Dataset family
none from the same source task
Overall verdict
fails task construction
New case assumed
New periods or event groups.
Split evidence
the curators state the 512-hour cases are non-overlapping segments of one 1967-2003 NCEDC record S; how the windows were assigned to train and test is not documented, and windows of one seismic sequence can sit on both sides R.
Label
classes defined by the curators' thresholds and exclusion rules (a reading over 5 not preceded by another within 512 hours; negatives below 4 with at least 20 non-zero readings in the previous 512 hours), as stated on the archive page S.
Open questions
Related periods (windows of one seismic record); whether this counts as clean is undecided.

New appliances or households
Exact duplicates
208 of 7,711 test series (2.7%) exactly match a training series D; 54 duplicate groups have conflicting labels
Why
The source kept each household in one set, but the shipped files differ in size from the published split, so it is unverified that the archive split keeps this. The value check finds 208 test cases identical to training cases, and 54 repeated-value groups carry conflicting labels. Most matches are two-valued on/off days, so they need not mean shared households.

Source

The Visual Energy Trial of the smart-meter company Green Energy Options (GEO) metered individual devices and total household electricity use in 187 homes across East Anglia. Readings were 15-minute consumption totals collected for about a year per household.

EOGHorizontalSignal EOG · Human motion & gesture · 362/362 train/test · length 1250 · 12 classes real tasksource task label Unclearassumed new case: new people or recording occasions temporaltime single channelfamily: eogopen question

Taskreal task

Source task
real task which of 12 Japanese Katakana stroke types was eye-written
Archive task
which of 12 Japanese Katakana stroke types was eye-written
Label attached by
Source study source task S
Verdict
real task

The source study posed a classification target that a stakeholder outside the paper needed.

SplitUnclear

New case assumed
New people or recording occasions
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The eye-movement samples come from six people. The audit describes splitting individual movements rather than keeping all movements from a person together. We cannot establish performance on unfamiliar users. Sharing users can suit a personalised system, but its test should explain whether they were measured again on a separate occasion.

Source

Fang & Shinozaki (2018) built an electrooculography eye-writing interface for ALS patients who can still move their eyes; each series is the horizontal channel of one isolated Japanese Katakana stroke from six participants.

Publication
Fang, Fuming, and Takahiro Shinozaki, "Electrooculography-based continuous eye-writing recognition system for efficient assistive communication systems", PLoS ONE 13(2): e0192684, 2018. Read 2026-08 via the PLOS article page. Methods: "A two-channel (horizontal and vertical) EOG signal was obtained by taking the differences of the signals between the electrodes on the left and right sides and those between the upper and lower sides, respectively"; the amplifier is a "commercial biomedical amplifier, BlueGain (Cambridge Research Systems Ltd.)"; six healthy participants (one female, five male), aged 21-31, one of whom was an eye-writing expert; Data recording > Isolated data: "In general, each of the 12 types of strokes was recorded 10 times for each participant, but a few strokes were recorded nine or 11 times. In total, 724 strokes were recorded from all the participants." Setup for isolated eye-writing recognition: "We used 10-fold cross-validation for performance evaluation" and "Both the DTW template and HMM were user-dependent" (how the folds were formed is not stated).
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-1250 single-channel EOG series (horizontal (left-right) eye movement) into one of 12 Katakana stroke types. 362 train / 362 test.
Who would use it
People with severe motor impairment (ALS, locked-in syndrome) and the clinicians /engineers building assistive communication devices for them.
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (which of 12 Japanese Katakana stroke types was eye-written); label origin: source task.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
channel-selectionwindowingz-normalization
Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
Archive default split; who built it and by what rule is not documented R R
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded two-channel electrooculography recorded around the left eye; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. The signal is intrinsically two-dimensional - eye-writing a stroke is a planar movement, and the horizontal and vertical channels are the two coordinates of it. This dataset keeps only the horizontal (left-right) component, so strokes that differ mainly along the other axis become indistinguishable in principle. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Dataset family
eog with EOGVerticalSignal

the horizontal and vertical channels of the same recordings, identical split

Overall verdict
real task; leakage unknown
New case assumed
New people or recording occasions.
Split evidence
six participants recorded 724 isolated strokes in total (Fang & Shinozaki 2018, "Data recording"), and the archive's 362 + 362 = 724, so train and test together hold all the isolated strokes S. How the strokes were divided between train and test, and whether the same participants appear on both sides, is not documented; the archive ships no participant identifier R. The source's own isolated-stroke models were user-dependent, evaluated by 10-fold cross-validation S; an assistive interface used by a new person would need a participant-disjoint split R. Hence Unclear R.
Open questions
How the 724 strokes were divided between train and test, and whether participants are shared, is not documented; matching the archive series against the source's S1 Database (isolated data) could settle it.

Permalink · CSV row: EOGHorizontalSignal

Publication
"Ford Classification Challenge", the competition's own website (home.comcast.net/~nn_classification/, dead since ~2016; read via the Internet Archive snapshot of 2008-07-06). Challenge section: "Data samples from an automotive subsystem were collected in batches of 500 samples per diagnostic session. On this dataset specifically: "Ford_A: Data samples of known and hidden classification were collected in typical operating conditions, with minimal noise contamination." The page's table gives Ford_A: 3271 training, 330 validation, 1320 test patterns — 3271 + 330 = 3601, exactly the archive's n_train, so UCR merged the competition's train and validation sets and kept its test set.

Source

The data come from the REFIT electrical load measurements study, which continuously monitored whole-house aggregate load plus nine individual appliances in each of 20 UK households at 8-second intervals over two years.

Publication
REFIT project ("Personalised Retrofit Decision Support Tools for UK Homes using Smart Home Technology"); primary data descriptor read: Murray, Stankovic & Stankovic, "An electrical load measurements dataset of United Kingdom households from a two-year longitudinal study", Scientific Data 4, 160122 (2017), Abstract: "The REFIT electrical load measurements dataset described in this paper includes whole house aggregate loads and nine individual appliance measurements at 8-second intervals per house, collected continuously over a period of two years from 20 houses." Curation described in Dau et al., "The UCR Time Series Archive", arXiv:1810.07758, Sec. VI-B-5 ("Electrical Load Measurement data - Freezer data sets"): "All data are from freezers in House 1. This data set has two classes, one representing the power demand of the fridge freezer in the kitchen, the other representing the power demand of the (less frequently used) freezer in the garage."

Fungi OTHER · Biology & ecology · 18/186 train/test · length 201 · 18 classes real tasksource task label Concernshared source ordered, non-temporaltemperature (melt curve) open question

Taskreal task

Source task
real task fungal species (18 species; Candida, Cryptococcus, Aspergillus and related genera)
Archive task
fungal species (18 species; Candida, Cryptococcus, Aspergillus and related genera)
Label attached by
Source study source task S
Verdict
real task

The source study posed a classification target that a stakeholder outside the paper needed.

SplitConcern

New case assumed
New biological strains
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The source measured fungal strains repeatedly. The audit reports that testing includes another measurement of a strain used in training. A model could recognise that familiar strain. This is a narrower test than identifying the species of a newly collected strain, for which all measurements of a strain should be kept together.

Source

A clinical-microbiology diagnostics problem - fungal infections need rapid, sensitive species identification, and high-resolution melt (HRM) analysis of a PCR-amplified DNA region is a cheap molecular assay whose curve shape is species-specific.

Publication
Lu, Mirchevska, Phatak, Li, Luka, Calderone, Fonzi, "Dynamic time warping assessment of high-resolution melt curves provides a robust metric for fungal identification", PLoS ONE 12(3), e0173320, 2017 (open access, read). Methods, "PCR amplification" - "Samples were then heated from 60-95 C at a r[ate] ... [with] acquisition of 25 fluorescent readings per 1 C. All samples were tested in duplicate in two independent trials." Methods, "High-resolution melt curve analysis" - "melting rates were directly determined using the first derivative of the normalized melt curves. Melt curve data was normalized within the LightCycler 480 Gene Scanning Software (Roche). The region between 72 and 74 C was set at 100%, 93.5 to 94 C was set to 0%. No curve shifting was applied. ... Rate curves were fitted with a cubic smoothing spline ... and the splines were used to calculate rates at 0.1 C increments. The resulting curves were z-normalized using the scale function and the region between 76 and 94 C was used for distance calculations." Introduction - "The sequentially ordered data of a melt curve is comparable to the temporally ordered data of a time series", i.e. the authors themselves frame the time-series treatment as an analogy.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a univariate length-201 melt-rate curve into one of 18 fungal species.
Who would use it
clinical microbiology laboratories identifying the fungal species causing an infection
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (fungal species (18 species; Candida, Cryptococcus, Aspergillus and related genera)); label origin: source task.

Data origin
ordered, non-temporal index: temperature (melt curve)

Source

A human-computer-interaction study of mid-air command gestures: 13 people drew each of 26 stroke-like commands (check, delete, circle, star, swipes, brackets, 3D polylines, ...) in the air, and a Leap Motion controller tracked the dominant-hand forefinger, giving a 3D trajectory per gesture.

Publication
Caputo, Prebianca, Carcangiu, Spano, Giachetti, "Comparing 3D trajectories for simple mid-air gesture recognition", Computers & Graphics 73 (2018) 17-25. Paper not read (paywalled, ScienceDirect returns HTTP 403). Layer 1 rests on the archive description page plus the authors' data/code repository https://github.com/giach68/gesturesCodes, which was inspected directly.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a variable-length series of one spatial coordinate of a mid-air fingertip trajectory into 26 gesture classes, training on 8 subjects and testing on 5 disjoint subjects.
Who would use it
designers of mid-air gestural interfaces (touchless control of displays, VR/AR applications)
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (which of 26 mid-air interface-command gestures was drawn); label origin: source task.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
channel-selection
Leakage
pass source separation documented
Split sources
Archive page: 'Data of a same subjects does not appear in both train and test set' (8 subjects train, 5 test; the source repository lists the reverse assignment) S RS
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded 3D position trajectory of the dominant-hand forefinger captured by a Leap Motion controller; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. Two of the three spatial coordinates are discarded, which is severe for a stroke-shape task: several of the 26 gestures (e.g. the six poly3D variants) differ only in the ordering of movements across axes. The explicit timestamp column of the raw data is dropped, so the index is treated as uniform time. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Dataset family
gesture-midair with GestureMidAirD2, GestureMidAirD3

D1, D2 and D3 are the x, y and z coordinates of the same fingertip trajectories

Overall verdict
real task with a documented split
New case assumed
New people R.
Split evidence
archive page: 'Data of a same subjects does not appear in both train and test set' (8 subjects train, 5 test) S; the repository lists the reverse assignment R.

The source study posed a classification target that a stakeholder outside the paper needed.

SplitConcern

New case assumed
New people
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The same four users performed gestures on two occasions; training uses the first occasion and testing the second (Dau et al. 2019). Every test user is also in training, so the score can rest on per-user motion signatures and does not measure recognition for new wearers of the watch; GesturePebbleZ2 is the subject-disjoint version.

Source

Mezari and Maglogiannis studied whether a commodity smartwatch can recognise natural hand gestures well enough to drive everyday device control, using the watch as the sensor and a phone as the processing unit (abstract, read in full: "Motion data produced by the accelerometer of a smart watch can be utilized in hand gesture re…

Publication
Mezari, Maglogiannis, "Gesture recognition using symbolic aggregate approximation and dynamic time warping on motion data", PervasiveHealth/PETRA (11th EAI Int. Conf. on Pervasive Computing Technologies for Healthcare), ACM 2017. Full text not read (closed access; OpenAlex reports no OA copy); abstract read in full via the OpenAlex API. The collection details above come from the UCR briefing document, which was read.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a variable-length z-axis wrist-accelerometer series as one of six gestures, under the split described above.
Who would use it
smartwatch-based gesture control of devices; the source group works on pervasive computing for healthcare, and the paper appeared at a health-oriented pervasive computing conference
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (which of six wrist gestures was performed); label origin: source task.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
channel-selection
Leakage
fail documented or data-verified shared source
Split sources
Dau et al. 2019 (p. 10): train = recording session 1, test = session 2 of the same four users S S
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded three-axis acceleration from a Pebble smartwatch worn on the wrist; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. Two of the three accelerometer axes are dropped, so gestures distinguished mainly by movement in the other planes lose their discriminating evidence; gesture duration is preserved (variable length) but the NaN padding must be handled by the user. Subject and session identity are not released as fields, only implied by the split. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Why
All 200 series come from one recording session of two actors, so train and test necessarily share that session and the split cannot test newly collected recordings. Which actor is on which side is not recorded. Having only two actors also limits how broadly results can be interpreted.

Source

A gesture-recognition study: two actors (one male, one female) either draw a replica gun from a hip holster and aim, or merely point with the index finger. Video tracking extracts the centroid of the right hand; the task is to tell the two gestures apart from the motion trajectory.

Publication
Ratanamahatana & Keogh (donors), UCR archive, ~2003-2005
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-150 hand-motion series as gun-draw or point.
Who would use it
Nominal surveillance motivation ("video surveillance domain"); no user is named, and the data were presumably created as a research benchmark R, so the "original problem" is already TSC-shaped.
Task construction
fail toy source task

Benchmark target: Gun-draw vs pointing. Source target or purpose: gesture performed (gun-draw vs point), staged at UCR by the method developers. Label attached by: Collectors (= curators). Mechanism 'toy source task': the data were simulated or staged by method developers and the classes chosen to demonstrate a method, not to answer an external question.

Data origin
temporal index: time

channel-selectionwindowingz-normalization
Leakage
fail documented or data-verified shared source
Split sources
Archive default split, 50 train / 150 test; who built it and by what rule is undocumented (the source evaluated by leave-one-out over all 200) R SR
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded video of the actor and tracked the right-hand centroid in X and Y; the source authors themselves posed the task on the X-coordinate only, so the release keeps one channel of a two-channel trajectory. The underlying video is richer than the released series; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded video of the two actors performing gun-draw and point actions; the released signal is the x-coordinate of the hand centroid per frame; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. The 2D hand trajectory is reduced to its x-coordinate and the rest of the video is discarded. Merging the 2003 and 2018 sessions adds a second source of variation (equipment, tracking, 15 years of the actors) that is inside the data rather than controlled. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Dataset family
gunpoint with GunPoint, GunPointAgeSpan, GunPointMaleVersusFemale

the 2003 GunPoint recording and the 2018 re-recording of the same two actors, pooled and relabelled three ways

Overall verdict
fails task construction and leakage
New case assumed
New recordings for each age condition.
Split evidence
the class is the recording session, so every train and test series of a class is a slice of the same session; no independent unit exists S.
Label
as GunPointMaleVersusFemale: the session task was posed by the people who recorded both sessions S. The session label (2003 vs 2018) was posed by the original collectors, who are also the archive's curators; equipment and recording-condition differences between the two sessions are available to the model S.

Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded finger trajectory on a touchscreen while entering a graphical password; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. A passgraph entry is a 2D gesture; only the X coordinate is released, so the drawn shape is only half observed and two different passgraphs that differ mainly in Y become indistinguishable. Pressure and timing metadata, which is what makes touch biometrics work in the literature, are not present either. Z-normalisation removes absolute screen position and stroke scale. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

none from the same source task

New recording occasions.

five users with repeated entries; no held-out-session evaluation is documented, so whether the test presents a new session of a known user is unknown R.
Label
Archive text: five people entering their passgraph; the label (user) is the biometric target the collection implies; no source documents this, so it is our reading, not a sourced fact I. No primary source; everything rests on one archive sentence. Whether the five users entered the same passgraph is undocumented; if not, the classifier may recognise the drawn shape rather than the person R.
Open questions
Authentication recast as five-way identification (archive page only); counted as instance identity as label. Whether the five users drew the same passgraph is undocumented.

Multichannel source

The source recorded surface EMG of seven leg muscles plus kinematic measurement of the right body side during treadmill inline speed skating; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. The original recording is multivariate (7 EMG channels plus kinematics); only a single kinematic channel, the ankle angle, survives - so the muscle-activation content that motivated the original study is entirely absent. Truncation to a common length cuts the end off longer movement cycles, which biases against slower cycles, and z-normalisation removes the joint-angle range of motion. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

InsectWingbeatSound SENSOR · Biology & ecology · 220/1,980 train/test · length 256 · 11 classes real tasksource task label Unclearassumed new case: new insect groups or recordings ordered, non-temporalfrequency (power spectrum) family: insect-wingbeatopen question

Taskreal task

Source task
real task insect species and sex from the wingbeat sound (11 classes)
Archive task
insect species and sex from the wingbeat sound (11 classes)
Label attached by
Source study source task S
Verdict
real task

The source study posed a classification target that a stakeholder outside the paper needed.

SplitUnclear

New case assumed
New insect groups or recordings
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The source recorded insects in groups inside cages. Cases from one group can share its insects and recording conditions. The files do not identify those groups, so we cannot check whether a whole group was kept in one set. Shared groups are possible, but have not been verified.

Source

UCR's computational entomology group built cheap pseudo-acoustic optical sensors — a laser line pointed at a phototransistor array — that record the light fluctuations caused by an insect's wings occluding the beam as it flies through.

Publication
Chen, Why, Batista, Mafra-Neto & Keogh, "Flying Insect Classification with Inexpensive Sensors", J. Insect Behavior 27(5):657-677, 2014 (arXiv:1403.2654, read in full). On the sensor: "design of the sensor consists of a phototransistor array which is connected to an electronic board, and a laser line pointing at the phototransistor array. When an insect flies across the laser beam, its wings partially occlude the light, causing small light fluctuations." On why the frequency domain: "The vast majority of attempts to classify insects by their flight sounds have explicitly or implicitly used just the wingbeat frequency... The Bayes error rate to classify the three species in Figure 1.I using just the wingbeat frequency is 19.13%; however, as we shall see below... by using the additional features from the signal, we can obtain [better results]." The detector stage is "a nearest neighbor classifier based on the frequency spectrum". That the ARCHIVE series are the spectrum rather than the raw sound is stated by the secondary dataset documentation (fda.tsc R package, InsectWingbeatSoundTSC: "The data in the archive is the power spectrum of the sound"; 2200 curves of 256 points, first 220 train) and by the timeseriesclassification.com description of the sibling InsectWingbeat dataset; the UCR description.php page for InsectWingbeatSound itself renders empty (checked 2026-08-18).
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a univariate length-256 power spectrum of an insect flight sound into one of 11 species/sex classes.
Who would use it
public-health vector control (mosquito surveillance) and agricultural pest monitoring
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (insect species and sex from the wingbeat sound (11 classes)); label origin: source task.

Data origin
ordered, non-temporal index: frequency (power spectrum)

Lightning2 SENSOR · Earth & space science · 60/61 train/test · length 637 · 2 classes real tasksource task label Unclearassumed new case: new recorded events temporaltime family: lightningopen question

Taskreal task

Source task
real task lightning transient type (seven expert classes in the source pipeline); Sec. 5 also poses cloud-to-ground vs intra-cloud
Archive task
cloud-to-ground vs intra-cloud lightning event (inferred; the archive does not name the two classes)
Label attached by
Source study source task SR
Verdict
real task

The source study posed a classification target that a stakeholder outside the paper needed.

SplitUnclear

New case assumed
New recorded events
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
Each series is one recorded lightning event, but no source documents how the 121 records were divided or whether events of one flash or storm sit on both sides. The source paper describes a two-class cloud-to-ground vs intra-cloud subproblem of its 143 records, evaluated by ten-fold cross-validation; dropping its 22 off-record events leaves 121, Lightning2's size, so Lightning2 is likely that set, but its fixed split is not documented. The split cannot be checked.

Source

Los Alamos researchers working with the FORTE (Fast On-orbit Recording of Transient Events) satellite needed to sort the enormous stream of recorded electromagnetic transients into physical event types (cloud-to-ground return strokes, intra-cloud events, and so on) automatically.

Publication
The archive description is the donor's own account of the preprocessing: "Data is collected with a sample rate of 50 MHz for 800 microseconds. A Fourier transform is performed on the input data to produce a spectrogram. The spectrograms are then collapsed in frequency to produce a power density time series, with 3181 samples in each time series. These are then smoothed to produce series of length 637." The underlying study is Eads, Hill, Davis, Perkins, Ma, Porter & Theiler, "Genetic algorithms and support vector machines for time series classification", Proc. SPIE 4787, 2002. That paper was read (November 2026): it describes 143 records in seven classes and, in Sec. 5, a two-class subproblem, cloud-to-ground (CG, IR, SR) vs intra-cloud (I, I2, KM) events, evaluated by ten-fold cross-validation; it gives no fixed train/test split.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-637 power-density trace of a FORTE transient into one of two classes, most likely cloud-to-ground vs intra-cloud events.
Who would use it
atmospheric-science and nuclear-test-monitoring programmes that must distinguish natural lightning transients from other RF events
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The source study classified FORTE transients into seven expert lightning types and posed a two-class subproblem, cloud-to-ground (CG, IR, SR) vs intra-cloud (I, I2, KM) events (Eads et al. 2002, Sec. 5). Without the 22 off-record events (implied by the Sec. 4 confusion matrices) its 143 records leave 121, Lightning2's size, so the label is taken as that source task R; the archive itself does not name the classes ('Not exactly sure what the two classes are').

Data origin
temporal index: time

Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
Archive default split; who built it and by what rule is not documented R R
Multichannel source
no
Dataset family
lightning with Lightning7

the same FORTE campaign and pipeline; Lightning2's two classes are taken as the source's cloud-to-ground vs intra-cloud grouping of Lightning7's seven classes without off-record events (inferred)

Overall verdict
real task; leakage unknown
New case assumed
New recorded events.
Split evidence
each series is one recorded FORTE transient; no source documents how the split was built or whether events of one flash sit on both sides R. The archive's own description says 'Not exactly sure what the two classes are'; the label is taken as the source's two-class subproblem, cloud-to-ground vs intra-cloud events (Eads et al. 2002, Sec. 5) S, matched by record count R.
Open questions
The archive does not name the two classes; reading them as the source's cloud-to-ground vs intra-cloud subproblem rests on the matching record count (121) R, and the class sizes (48/73) differ by one from those the source's seven classes imply (49/72).

Source

FORTE satellite recordings of VHF electromagnetic transients from lightning, each labelled by domain experts with its physical discharge type and used at Los Alamos to drive a genetic-programming feature extractor.

Publication
The archive description is the donor's own account of both the preprocessing and the class physics: "Data is collected with a sample rate of 50 MHz for 800 microseconds. A Fourier transform is performed on the input data to produce a spectrogram. The spectrograms are then collapsed in frequency to produce a power density time series, with 3181 samples in each time series. These are then smoothed to produce series of length 637." On the classes, e.g.: "IR Negative Initial Return Stroke... The power waveform slowly ramps up to a level known as an attachment point, where a large surge current causes the VHF power to 'spike'. This attachment is followed by an exponentially shaped decline in the waveform." and "O Off-record: 800 microseconds was not enough to fully capture the lightning event." The underlying study is Eads, Hill, Davis, Perkins, Ma, Porter & Theiler, Proc. SPIE 4787, 2002; it was read (November 2026): Sec. 2.3 gives the seven classes and 143 records, Sec. 4.1 a ten-fold random cross-validation; it gives no fixed train/test split.

The source study posed automatic annotation of historical images (linking each to a modern counterpart with its meta tags), a task digital libraries indexing scanned collections needed.

SplitConcern

New case assumed
New images or physical objects
Exact duplicates
1 of 2,425 test series (0%) exactly match a training series D
Why
The cases are outlines from shape images. We do not know whether several views or processed versions of one original object can occur in both sets. If they do, the model may recognise a familiar shape. The source-image information needed to check this is not described in the audit. Separately, the value check reports one test case identical to training data.

Source

Automatic annotation of images in digitized historical archives (Wang et al., JCDL 2008): outlines of arrowheads, butterflies, fish, seashells and shields, the kind of object in the source's natural-history plates and artefacts (the source does not itemise this set).

Publication
Wang, Ye, Keogh & Shelton, "Annotating historical archives of images", JCDL 2008 (read; cited as [1] by the archive page), together with Keogh et al., "LB_Keogh supports exact indexing of shapes under rotation invariance with arbitrary representations and distance measures", VLDB 2006 (cited as [2], not read). Neither source itemises this five-class dataset as released, so the composition of the archive version rests on the archive page.

MoteStrain SENSOR · Industrial, sensors & robotics · 20/1,252 train/test · length 84 · 2 classes failsinstance identity as label Concernshared source temporaltime single channel

Taskfails

Source task
none Unsupervised stream correlation tracking; no class target
Archive task
Which source stream: humidity vs temperature
Label attached by
Curators instance identity S
Verdict
fails instance identity as label

The source collected the data but posed no classification target.

SplitConcern

New case assumed
Independent recordings
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The audit traces cases to nearby pieces of a small number of sensor recordings, with the recording defining the class. Training and testing therefore share those original recordings. The model may recognise their particular conditions. The test does not show that the distinction works across recordings collected independently.

Source

Calibrated humidity and temperature streams from a wireless sensor-mote deployment (CMU), used by Sun and Faloutsos as an unsupervised testbed for tracking correlations among co-evolving streams; Keogh later cut windows from two streams and used the stream identity as the class.

Publication
timeseriesclassification.com description page: "Sensor data originally from Carlos Guestrin (CMU) the subsequently used in the paper Online Latent Variable Detection in Sensor Networks and formatted for classification by Eamonn Keogh. The task is to distinguish between sensor q8calibHumid (a humidity measure) and sensor q8calibHumTemp (temperature). The data has dropouts. In the original data the dropouts have value 0. This means they are difficult to detect with normalised data." The linked paper — Sun, Papadimitriou & Faloutsos, "Online Latent Variable Detection in Sensor Networks", ICDE 2005 (demo, pp. 1126-1127) — was read in full; it describes the system and says the demo data source "consists of 8 sensor streams, 4 temperature streams and 4 light intensity streams" from "MICA2 wireless motes with MTS310 sensor boards", and does not mention humidity streams or the q8calib* files. The named files instead appear in the archive's second link (github.com/MrKriss/CDST-demo/tree/master/datasets/Motes), whose README was read: "q8calibHumid.dat - humidity measurements / q8calibHumTemp.dat - temperature measurements ... These datasets are produced by Jimeng Sun and Christos Faloutsos (CMU). The original data are provided by Carlos Guestrin (CMU)."
Full record
Benchmark task
Given a length-84 z-normalized sensor window, decide whether it came from the humidity stream or the temperature stream.
Who would use it
none in the applied sense — nobody needs a model to tell a humidity sensor from a thermometer, since the sensor is known at read time. The realistic use is diagnostic (detecting mislabelled or miswired channels in a sensor network).
Task construction
fail instance identity as label

Benchmark target: Which source stream: humidity vs temperature. Source target or purpose: Unsupervised stream correlation tracking; no class target. Label attached by: Curators. Mechanism 'instance identity as label': each class is one specific sensor stream.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
windowingz-normalization
Leakage
fail documented or data-verified shared source
Split sources
Archive default split; who built it and by what rule is not documented R SR
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording
Dataset family
none from the same source task
Overall verdict
fails task construction and leakage
New case assumed
Independent recordings.
Split evidence
windows of at most two long recordings; train and test windows are neighbouring 84-point segments of the same streams and the class is the stream itself R.
Label
archive page: 'formatted for classification by Eamonn Keogh'; the class is which file the window came from R. The class is which source file (humidity vs temperature stream) a window was cut from; the source study was unsupervised correlation tracking R.

Why
We read the labels as identifying recordings, with training and test heartbeats coming from those same recordings. However, the source mapping is not confirmed.
