Source

Thoracic ECG channels recorded alongside abdominal ones for non-invasive fetal ECG extraction (PhysioNet); the archive page does not identify the source and gives 42 classes with no key.

Publication
timeseriesclassification.com description page (which explicitly records the curators' own uncertainty about the source), PhysioNet/CinC Challenge 2013 page linked from it, and PhysioNet Non-Invasive Fetal ECG Database (https://physionet.org/content/nifecgdb/1.0.0/, read 2026-08). Who would use it
obstetricians / non-invasive fetal heart monitoring in pregnancy and labour
Task construction
pass none (label meaning undocumented)

Benchmark target: one of 42 classes with no documented meaning. The source posed no classification target and no source says what the archive's classes mean, so original task and label origin are recorded as undocumented. We do not count it among the constructed tasks, because no source shows that the archive constructed the label; it is counted with the real tasks and flagged as an open question.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
channel-selectionwindowingz-normalization
Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
Archive default split; who built it and by what rule is not documented R R
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded multichannel non-invasive ECG of a pregnant woman; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. The recordings are multichannel by design and the whole point of the modality is to combine abdominal and thoracic channels to cancel the maternal ECG. This dataset keeps a single thoracic channel (thorax 1) and presents it as a univariate series, so the fetal signal - the reason the data exists - is not recoverable from it at all. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Dataset family
nifecg with NonInvasiveFetalECGThorax2

the two thoracic channels of the same recordings, identical split

Overall verdict
label meaning undocumented (counted with the real tasks); leakage unknown
New case assumed
Independent recordings.
Split evidence
the archive does not identify the source record; if it is nifecgdb the 42 classes are sessions of one woman and every class's beats come from one recording, but that premise is unresolved, so class-internal recording sharing is not established R.
Label
a curator-assigned grouping over recordings whose meaning the archive itself does not know R. The 42 classes have no documented meaning; the reading that they are recording sessions of one subject (nifecgdb) is unverified R. Because the label meaning is undocumented, the dataset is not counted among the constructed tasks; original task and label origin are recorded as undocumented (policy L4).
Open questions
The 42 classes have no documented meaning; if they are sessions of one subject the label would be instance identity. Not counted among the constructed tasks.

Why
One spoken word can supply several phoneme segments. We do not know whether complete word recordings or speakers were kept in one set. Segments in both sets could share a voice and recording conditions. The test's use of new speakers or separately recorded words remains unverified.

Source

Large-scale phoneme classification for speech recognition (Hamooni & Mueen, ICDM 2014): 370,000 automatically segmented phoneme waveforms in 39 classes from 78,000 English words; the archive holds a small subsample.

Publication
Hamooni and Mueen, "Dual-domain Hierarchical Classification of Phonetic Time Series", IEEE ICDM 2014, Sec. III Data Collection and Cleaning (read) - "We have collected English words from three sources with their audio files. These consist of 30,000 words from Google Translate, 3,000 words from oxforddictionaries.com and 45000 words from the Merrriam-Webster online dictionary. ... recorded at 22050, 44100 and 11025 samples per second respectively." "After data collection, we segment waveforms of the words to generate phonemes. We use the Forced Aligner tool from the Penn Phonetics Laboratory, which segments a word based on its phonetic spelling in the dictionary." "The segmentation process generated 370,000 phonemes in 39 classes covering the entire dictionary." Sec. III-C - "we do a resampling of the words to have all phoneme waveforms at 22050 sample per seconds."
Full record

Classify a univariate length-1024 audio segment into 39 phoneme classes.
Who would use it
speech-recognition / pronunciation systems; the authors' motivation is a phoneme classifier that generalizes across recording sources rather than overfitting one corpus

Why
The labels identify people, with several gesture repetitions per person. We do not know whether those repetitions were recorded on separate occasions or how the occasions were assigned to the sets. The test may check more repetitions from a familiar recording. Identification after recording the person again is not established.

Source

Guna, Humar and Pogacnik proposed identifying a user from the way they handle a control device: a person picks up a Nintendo Wii remote from rest, and the three-axis accelerometer trace of that everyday movement is matched (with DTW) against enrolled templates to log the user in.

Publication
Guna, Humar, Pogacnik, "Intuitive gesture based user identification system", 35th Int. Conf. on Telecommunications and Signal Processing (TSP), IEEE 2012. Full text not read - the paper is closed access (OpenAlex reports oa_status "closed", no repository copy). Its abstract was read in full via the OpenAlex API: "We present an intuitive and very easy to use implicit gesture based identification system that is especially suited for security-wise non-critical applications, such as the user login in the multimedia services. The term 'implicit gesture' in our work refers to a natural physical hand manipulation performed by the user who takes hold of the control device and simply picks it up from its neutral motionless position - a 'pick-up' gesture... 100% recognition accuracy was achieved when using explicit identification signature gestures and over 91% recognition accuracy was achieved when using the implicit 'pick-up' gesture."
Full record
Benchmark task
Given a variable-length z-axis acceleration trace of one pick-up (poteg) gesture, decide which of ten people produced it.
Who would use it
implicit/low-friction user login for non-critical applications such as multimedia services (the source study's stated use case)
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
Archive default split; who built it and by what rule is not documented R R
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded three-axis acceleration from a hand-held Nintendo Wii remote during a single natural handling gesture; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. Two of three axes dropped; the other nine gestures each subject performed are split off into other data sets, and the archive states that "data are shuffled and randomly sampled so that instances across datasets do not sychronized by dimension or subject", so the per-subject multi-gesture structure that the source system actually used is not recoverable. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

real task; leakage unknown

label = subject; whether the ten repetitions were recorded in more than one session, and whether the split separates sessions, is undocumented R.

PigAirwayPressure HEMODYNAMICS · Physiological signals · 104/208 train/test · length 2000 · 52 classes failsinstance identity as label Concernshared source temporaltime single channelfamily: pig-hemodynamics

The source study posed its own prediction task, which the archive did not keep as the label.

SplitConcern

New case assumed
New recording occasions
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The task is to identify pigs. Training uses the first pieces of each pig's before/after pressure recordings; testing uses later pieces of the same recordings (Dau et al. 2019). The same pigs are expected in an identification task. The concern is that the model is tested on familiar recordings, rather than after measuring those pigs again.

Source

Early detection of internal bleeding after surgery, studied in an animal model. Guillame-Bert & Dubrawski (JMLR 2017), Sec. 7.4 "Internal Bleeding Detection Data Set", p. 18: "Undetected internal bleeding during and after surgical procedures is a major medical concern."

Publication
Guillame-Bert, Mathieu, and Artur Dubrawski, "Classification of Time Sequences using Graphs of Temporal Constraints", Journal of Machine Learning Research 18(1), 2017, 4370-4403. PDF read 2026-08 (Sec. 7.4, p. 18; Fig. 8 caption, p. 19).
Full record
Benchmark task
Given an 8-second window of airway pressure from a sedated pig, identify which of 52 animals it came from. 104 train (2 per pig) / 208 test (4 per pig).
Who would use it
Critical-care and trauma clinicians; the goal is an alarm that detects occult haemorrhage minutes rather than tens of minutes after onset.
Task construction
fail instance identity as label

Benchmark target: Which of 52 pigs. Source target or purpose: Before vs during induced bleeding. Label attached by: Curators. Mechanism 'instance identity as label': the archive uses the recording's source (subject, sensor or actor) as the class.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
channel-selectionwindowingz-normalization
Leakage
fail documented or data-verified shared source
Split sources
Dau et al. 2019 (p. 10) / archive page: train = the first 2000-point slice, test = the second and third 2000-point slices of each pig's before and after recordings S RS
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded bedside hemodynamic monitoring of a sedated pig; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. The source data is multivariate by construction - airway pressure, arterial pressure and central venous pressure were recorded simultaneously from the same animal - and the bleeding signature lives in their joint behaviour. This dataset presents airway pressure alone. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Dataset family
pig-hemodynamics with PigArtPressure, PigCVP

three simultaneously recorded channels of the same 52-pig experiment, identical classes and split sizes

Overall verdict
fails task construction and leakage
New case assumed
New recording occasions.
Split evidence
the 2 training and 4 test windows of a class are consecutive 8-second slices of the same two 30-second recordings of the same animal R.
Label
the archive authors built the three datasets from the donor's recordings and made each pig a class (Dau et al. 2019, Sec. VI-C4: 'We created three data sets out of this source') S. The 52-way pig-identity label replaced the source study's before/during-bleeding label (Dau et al. 2019: 'Class i is the ith individual pig'); the source authors note that between-animal differences dominate the bleeding contrast S.

Why
The source recorded individual appliances several times. We do not know whether all recordings of an appliance were kept in one set. Familiar appliances might occur in testing, but this is not established by the missing information. Separately, the value check finds five test cases identical to training cases.

Source

PLAID (Plug-Load Appliance Identification Dataset) was collected at Carnegie Mellon to support load identification / non-intrusive load monitoring research.

Publication
Gao, Giri, Kara, Berges, "PLAID: a public dataset of high-resolution electrical appliance measurements for load identification research: demo abstract", BuildSys 2014 (not read -- ACM DL returned 403, and the project site www.plaidplug.com is dead, now a parked domain). Read instead the open-access data descriptor for the same dataset family: Medico, De Baets, Gao, Giri, Kara, Dhaene, Develder, Berges, Deschrijver, "A voltage and current measurement dataset for plug load appliance identification in households", Scientific Data 7, 49 (2020).
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a variable-length, NaN-padded 30 kHz current waveform (537 train / 537 test) into one of 11 appliance types.
Who would use it
Utilities and demand-side-management / NILM vendors that must infer which appliances are running from electrical measurements; also smart-plug manufacturers.

pass none (source task label)

temporal index: time
Archive changes
windowingchannel-selection
Leakage
fail exact cross-split matches (5 of 537 test series)
Split sources
Archive default split; who built it and by what rule is not documented R DIR
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded high-frequency current and voltage waveforms measured at the plug during an appliance switch-on event; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. PLAID records both current and voltage; the univariate UCR version keeps a single channel, discarding the voltage reference that power/phase features are computed from. Records are event windows around a switch-on, so the startup transient is over-represented relative to normal operation. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Dataset family
none from the same source task

fails leakage
New case assumed
New physical appliances.
Split evidence
three to six records per appliance instance R; the split is not documented as instance-disjoint, so instance sharing is inferred from the absence of a grouping statement rather than observed; a NILM model meets new appliances I. The Concern verdict rests on the five exact cross-split matches D; appliance-instance sharing is inferred from the absence of a grouping statement, not observed I.
Open questions
Concern rests on the exact cross-split matches alone; appliance sharing is not established.

Why
The photographs show die-cast plane models, 30 shape samples per class; the source does not say how many physical models were photographed, nor how models or photography sessions were assigned to the two sets. The test could therefore contain another view of a familiar model; that possibility is not verified from the metadata.

Source

The companion database to Car, from the same computer-vision paper. Thakoor & Gao photographed die-cast replica fighter aircraft from directly above and segmented the silhouettes, in order to test a shape classifier on classes that are deliberately very similar to one another.

Publication
Thakoor & Gao, "Shape Classifier based on Generalized Probabilistic Descent Method with Hidden Markov Descriptor", ICCV 2005, Sec. 7.1 (read in full): "The fighter aeroplane shape database included Mirage, Eurofighter, F-14, Harrier, F-22 and F-15. Since F-14 has two possible shapes, one when its wings are closed and another when its wings are opened, total number of shape classes are seven. Each class includes 30 shape samples. Shape database was created by taking digital pictures of die-cast replica models of these aeroplanes from top. Pictures were captured at 640x480 resolution, and were segmented using Spedge and Medge color image segmentation algorithm. Contours of the segmented planes were used for training and testing of the classifier."
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a univariate length-144 series (an unrolled aircraft outline) into one of seven aircraft-type classes.
Who would use it
military/aerial surveillance systems classifying aircraft views; in practice a purpose-built vision benchmark

Nearby days can share weather and routines, and no source says whether a later period was kept for testing. Separately, 67 of 180 test cases exactly match training cases. These matches are a concrete issue beyond the possible relationship between neighbouring days.

Source

The underlying data are individual-household electric power consumption readings attributed by the archive to "EDF R&D, Clamart, France" -- i.e. the research arm of the French utility, which meters households to study consumption and support load forecasting and demand analysis.

Publication
timeseriesclassification.com description page only ("Source: EDF R&D, Clamart, France"; "The sampling rate is every ten-minute over a period of one year"). NO primary publication for PowerCons was located despite targeted searching. The UCI "Individual household electric power consumption" dataset was donated by Georges Hebrail and Alice Berard from the same EDF R&D Clamart group (UCI page read: one-minute readings from a house in Sceaux, December 2006 - November 2010) and is a plausible parent, but the sampling rate and the one-year span do not match, so the link is UNCONFIRMED and must not be treated as provenance.
Full record

Who would use it
Utility load analysts use household demand curves for forecasting and planning; nobody needs to infer the season from a load curve, since the date is always known -- the task is a curator-built proxy.

RefrigerationDevices DEVICE · Energy & appliances · 375/375 train/test · length 720 · 3 classes real tasksource task label Unclearassumed new case: new appliances or households temporaltime family: household-electricity-surveyopen question

Taskreal task

Source task
real task Household electricity monitoring for energy policy (Household Electricity Survey); each metered channel is annotated with its appliance, but the survey posed no classification task
Archive task
which refrigeration appliance produced the day's consumption trace (fridge/freezer, refrigerator, upright freezer)
Label attached by
Collectors source task SR
Verdict
real task

The survey's installing electricians annotated each monitored product with its appliance ("selected from a dropdown list"), so under policy L3 we count appliance-type recognition as the source's own task although the survey trained no classifier; the classification problem was posed on those annotations by the curators (Lines & Bagnall, SDM 2014; Lines 2015 thesis). Each input is an appliance trace metered on its own, so the task is appliance recognition, not whole-house disaggregation.

SplitUnclear

New case assumed
New appliances or households
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
Same as Computers. [Computers: The source measured each physical appliance over many days. We do not know whether all days from an appliance or household were kept in one set. If the same appliance appears in both sets, the model may recognise its usual pattern. That would be a narrower test than recognising appliance types in new homes.]

Source

These series come from the UK government-sponsored household electricity monitoring study known as "Powering the Nation" (the DECC/Defra/Energy Saving Trust Household Electricity Survey).

Publication
UK Household Electricity Survey (DECC, Defra and the Energy Saving Trust, 2010-2011); Intertek Report R66141, "Household Electricity Survey: A study of domestic electrical product usage", final report issue 4. Household counts differ: the archive page, the thesis and Intertek (p. 24) give 251 households, Report 5 (p. 4) and gov.uk give 250; the 26 year-long households were sampled every 10 minutes except from late November 2010 to February 2011, when they recorded every 2 minutes (Intertek p. 64). Curated into TSC datasets by Lines & Bagnall (UEA): J. Lines, PhD thesis, UEA, 2015, Sec. 3.2.2 and Table 3.3 ("We provide five new classification problems that focus on domestic appliance classification from electricity consumption"); introduced in Lines & Bagnall, "Ensembles of elastic distance measures for time series classification", SIAM SDM 2014 (not read: no full text found).
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a 720-point daily consumption profile as fridge/freezer, refrigerator or upright freezer.
Who would use it
UK energy policy (the survey); the curators' stated aim was to extract more from smart-meter data by "classifying appliances according to their electricity usage profiles". The derived task (naming a device from its own metered daily profile) is appliance recognition, not disaggregation of a whole-house load.
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the survey's appliance annotation of the metered channel (which refrigeration appliance produced the day's consumption trace (fridge/freezer, refrigerator, upright freezer)); label origin: source task, by the convention that a collectors' annotation of the quantity a deployed model would predict counts as the source task.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
windowingz-normalization
Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
Archive default split, made by the curators (Lines & Bagnall), who say these datasets were "split in a certain manner to avoid introducing bias" from one device appearing in both train and test S; the rule used is not stated and the files carry no household or device identifiers R IR
Multichannel source
no
Dataset family
household-electricity-survey with Computers, LargeKitchenAppliances, SmallKitchenAppliances, ScreenType

five appliance-group tasks cut from the UK Household Electricity Survey (ElectricDevices comes from a different study, the Visual Energy Trial); whether they share households or appliances is not documented

Overall verdict
real task; leakage unknown
New case assumed
New appliances or households.
Split evidence
each series is one day cut from a month (or, for some households, a year) of metering, so one appliance can supply many series R; which households, appliances and days entered the dataset is not documented. The curators say the split was made to avoid device bias, but give no rule, and the files carry no device identifiers, so device- or household-disjointness is neither documented nor refuted and sharing is not counted as leakage R. An appliance-recognition model meeting unseen appliances is the deployment case I.
Open questions
Appliance-type recognition is counted as the source task by convention (the survey annotated the channels but posed no classification task); a stricter reading files it as metadata. Appliance/household sharing inferred from the survey design but not documented, so Unclear rather than Concern.

Rock SPECTRO · Earth & space science · 20/50 train/test · length 2844 · 4 classes failsmetadata as label Unclearassumed new case: new physical samples ordered, non-temporalwavelength or wavenumber

Source task
none ASTER spectral-library metadata; no defined task
Archive task
Four rock types
Label attached by
Curators metadata SR
Verdict
fails metadata as label

The source collected the data but posed no classification target.

SplitUnclear

New case assumed
New physical samples
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
Our similarity check found strongly similar pairs of sequences. That alone does not show that the same physical rock was measured twice. We need sample identifiers to know whether the two sets share rocks. Until then, repeated measurements are a possibility, rather than confirmed overlap.

Source

There was no original prediction problem. The data come from the ASTER Spectral Library v2.0, a NASA/JPL reference collection built to support interpretation of ASTER satellite imagery.

Publication
Baldridge, Hook, Grove, Rivera, "The ASTER spectral library version 2.0", Remote Sensing of Environment 113(4), 711-715, 2009, doi:10.1016/j.rse.2008.11.007. Read in full (open PDF at speclib.jpl.nasa.gov/downloads/2009-Baldridge.pdf). It documents the spectra; it does not document the UCR extract - which 70 samples were taken, how the 20/50 split was made, and how the 2844 points relate to the library's two acquisition ranges (0.4-2.5 um and 2-15.4 um) are nowhere stated.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a univariate length-2844 reflectance spectrum as mafic, quartzite, marble or schist.
Who would use it
geologists and remote-sensing analysts use the library as a reference for mineral mapping; no one posed this 70-sample supervised classification
Task construction
fail metadata as label

Benchmark target: Four rock types. Source target or purpose: ASTER spectral-library metadata; no defined task. Label attached by: Curators. Mechanism 'metadata as label': the archive turns the library's rock-type metadata (Subclass field) into the class, for a source that posed no classification.

Data origin
ordered, non-temporal index: wavelength or wavenumber
Archive changes
spectrum-as-series
Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
Archive default split; who built it and by what rule is not documented R DR
Multichannel source
no
Dataset family
none from the same source task
Overall verdict
fails task construction (leakage unknown)
New case assumed
New physical samples.
Split evidence
near-duplicate pairs cross the split (r > 0.99) and the test set holds one exact duplicate pair; replicate leakage is plausible but unproven for near-single-mineral rocks R+D.
Label
four rock classes chosen by the archive contributor (Y. Zhu) from the ASTER spectral library's metadata; the library's authors posed no classification R. The four classes were chosen by the archive contributor (Y. Zhu, a UCR archive co-author) from ASTER library metadata; filed as Curators R.

SemgHandGenderCh2 SPECTRO · Physiological signals · 300/600 train/test · length 1500 · 2 classes failsmetadata as label Concernshared source ordered, non-temporalfrequency (power spectrum) single channelfamily: semg-handopen question

Taskfails

Source task
real task Six hand-grasp types
Archive task
Sex of five subjects
Label attached by
Curators metadata S
Verdict
fails metadata as label

The source study posed its own prediction task, which the archive did not keep as the label.

SplitConcern

New case assumed
New people
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
We found the same five people in both sets, checked using labels from the related person-identification dataset. Recognising a familiar person could help predict their gender label. The test checks further measurements of these people, rather than establishing performance on people absent from training.

Source

Surface EMG of the forearm recorded by Sapsanis et al. (2013) from five healthy subjects performing six everyday grasps, to build myoelectric grasp recognition for prosthetic or assistive hands; the archive replaces each repetition by its power spectrum.

Publication
Sapsanis, Georgoulas, Tzes, Lymberopoulos, "Improving EMG based classification of basic hand movements using EMD", IEEE EMBC 2013 - not read (IEEE Xplore, paywalled); only its title and the archive's citation of it. The recording protocol above is quoted from the UCI Machine Learning Repository page for the dataset, which was read. The archive's own processing step ("We extract the power spectrum from the raw data") is stated on the timeseriesclassification.com page and in the README shipped inside the dataset zip, but is documented nowhere in more detail.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a univariate length-1500 sEMG power spectrum as produced by a female or a male subject.
Who would use it
prosthetics and assistive-device researchers building myoelectric grasp control; rehabilitation and HCI groups
Task construction
fail metadata as label

Benchmark target: Sex of five subjects. Source target or purpose: Six hand-grasp types. Label attached by: Curators. Mechanism 'metadata as label': the archive derives the classes from a recording attribute.

Data origin
ordered, non-temporal index: frequency (power spectrum)

Index is frequency: the archive replaces each six-second EMG repetition by its power spectrum, so the ordered axis is spectral, not time.

Archive changes
channel-selectionspectrum-as-series
Leakage
fail documented or data-verified shared source
Split sources
Undocumented 300/600 split of the 900 series, unequal per subject (train 57/41/52/77/73, test 123/139/128/103/107) D; one recording session per subject R DR
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded surface EMG recording from two forearm electrodes during a hand grasp; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. The Fourier transform discards all phase and all temporal structure: burst onset, contraction timing, the ramp of the grasp, and every relation between the two forearm channels (only one is kept). What remains is an order-free energy-by-frequency profile - the archive did the feature extraction that an EMG practitioner would have done, and then presented the features as a "time series". The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Dataset family
semg-hand with SemgHandMovementCh2, SemgHandSubjectCh2

the same 900 series (byte-identical), labelled by grasp, by subject or by sex

Overall verdict
fails task construction and leakage
New case assumed
New people.
Split evidence
five subjects and every subject appears on both sides (verified by joining the SemgHandSubjectCh2 labels by series hash); a sex classifier is deployed on new people R+D.
Label
sex label attached by the archive editor (C. Yeh) from subject metadata; the source study's target was the grasp R. The 1500-point series is the power spectrum of one 6-second grasp repetition (index = frequency); filed as ordered, non-temporal. Subject sharing across the split was verified by joining the SemgHandSubjectCh2 labels by series hash D.
Open questions
Index is frequency; a reader who regards a power spectrum as a time axis would count it as temporal.

The Fourier transform discards all phase and all temporal structure: burst onset, contraction timing, the ramp of the grasp, and every relation between the two forearm channels (only one is kept). What remains is an order-free energy-by-frequency profile - the archive did the feature extraction that an EMG practitioner would have done, and then presented the features as a "time series". The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Dataset family
semg-hand with SemgHandGenderCh2, SemgHandMovementCh2

the same 900 series (byte-identical), labelled by grasp, by subject or by sex

Overall verdict
fails task construction and leakage
New case assumed
New recording occasions.
Split evidence
the archive page defines the classes as the five subjects, so every subject is on both sides S; each subject was recorded once, so the split is a stratified shuffle of one session per subject and the test presents no new session of a known subject R.
Label
subject-identity label attached by the archive editor, stated on the archive page S; not posed in the source study R. The 1500-point series is the power spectrum of one 6-second grasp repetition (index = frequency); filed as ordered, non-temporal.
Open questions
Index is frequency; a reader who regards a power spectrum as a time axis would count it as temporal.

Source

Guna, Humar and Pogacnik proposed identifying a user from the way they handle a control device: a person picks up a Nintendo Wii remote from rest, and the three-axis accelerometer trace of that everyday movement is matched (with DTW) against enrolled templates to log the user in.

Publication
Guna, Humar, Pogacnik, "Intuitive gesture based user identification system", 35th Int. Conf. on Telecommunications and Signal Processing (TSP), IEEE 2012. Full text not read - the paper is closed access (OpenAlex reports oa_status "closed", no repository copy). Its abstract was read in full via the OpenAlex API: "We present an intuitive and very easy to use implicit gesture based identification system that is especially suited for security-wise non-critical applications, such as the user login in the multimedia services. The term 'implicit gesture' in our work refers to a natural physical hand manipulation performed by the user who takes hold of the control device and simply picks it up from its neutral motionless position - a 'pick-up' gesture... 100% recognition accuracy was achieved when using explicit identification signature gestures and over 91% recognition accuracy was achieved when using the implicit 'pick-up' gesture."

Source

Generated by the UEA group (Hills, Bagnall) as a probe for shapelet algorithms: a class is defined by one short discriminative shape inserted at a random position in white noise; no real-world problem exists.

Publication
Bagnall, Lines, Vickers et al., "Simulated Data Experiments for Time Series Classification Part 1 - Accuracy Comparison with Default Settings", arXiv:1703.09480, Sec. 5 "Shapelet Simulator" (read) - "A shapelet is a discriminatory pattern embedded in a series. Currently this simulator only works for a single shapelet defining a class. Each model randomly chooses a shapelet from those described in Figure ??. The shapelet is placed at a random location for each series from that model (by the call to generateSeries(int length)), embedded in noise." The design intent is stated in Sec. 5.1 - "we think vector based classifiers would be poor at these data, because the random location of the discriminatory shape should confound them ... shapelet classifiers should be the best". The archive page names the same generator class (SimulateShapeletDataset) and the shape library (TRIANGLE, HEADSHOULDERS, SINE, STEP, SPIKE). Hills, Lines, Baranauskas, Mapp, Bagnall, "Classification of time series by shapelet transformation", DMKD 28(4), 2014 - the paper the donors are known for - was not reached (the CORE mirror returned HTTP 522, the Springer copy is paywalled), so the parameters used for the archived copy in particular are undocumented.
Full record

Source

No real-world problem exists. The data was generated by Huang et al. to test a clustering algorithm - specifically whether a k-means variant can recover "smooth subspaces", i.e. contiguous stretches of time stamps that carry the cluster signal while the remaining stamps are noise.

Publication
Huang, Ye, Xiong, Lau, Jiang, Wang, "Time series k-means - A new k-means type smooth subspace clustering for time series data", Information Sciences 367-368 (2016) 1-13. The primary paper was not reached (ScienceDirect paywall, article S0020025516303796); the generator description comes from the archive page, which was written by the data editor and cites that paper - "The data was originally intended for testing whether a clustering algorithm is able to extract smooth subspaces for clustering time series data [1]. There are 3 classes corresponding to which cluster the time series belong to. Each time series contain a continuous subspace spanning over 5 continuous time stamps. ... The rest of the time series are randomly generated. Data created by Xiaohui Huang et al. (see [1]). Data edited by Hoang Anh Dau."
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a univariate length-15 series into the three generated cluster classes.
Who would use it
none (synthetic demo)

SonyAIBORobotSurface1 SENSOR · Industrial, sensors & robotics · 20/601 train/test · length 70 · 2 classes real tasksource task label Concernshared source temporaltime single channelfamily: sony-aibo

Taskreal task

Source task
real task walking surface under the robot (cement vs carpet)
Archive task
walking surface under the robot (cement vs carpet)
Label attached by
Source study source task S
Verdict
real task

The source study posed a classification target that a stakeholder outside the paper needed.

SplitConcern

New case assumed
New recording occasions
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The audit describes short pieces from one continuous robot recording per surface. Training and testing therefore share the robot run and its recording conditions. The test checks more pieces from those runs. It does not show whether surface recognition works after collecting another recording on a separate occasion.

Source

Vail & Veloso wanted a legged robot to recognize what it is walking on, so that it can switch to a gait parameter set tuned for that surface.

Publication
Vail & Veloso, "Learning from Accelerometer Data on a Legged Robot", 5th IFAC/EURON Symposium on Intelligent Autonomous Vehicles, 2004 (read in full). Sec. 1.1: "In addition to its camera, the robot also has an accelerometer. The accelerometer returns a real valued estimate of the robot's acceleration along the x, y, and z axes. The values are limited to the range of [-2,2] gravities for each axis and are sampled at 125 hertz. We define positive x as the direction that the robot is facing". Sec. 2: "We present experimental results showing the robots performance discriminating between a cement floor, the carpet in our laboratory, and the carpet on a RoboCup field." Sec. 2.2: "To test our surface prediction algorithm, we gathered five minute segments of accelerometer readings while the robot marched in place on each surface." Sec. 2.1: "Since the data arrives as a stream, we window it into discrete chunks when creating the feature vector for the classifier... We chose to use a one second sliding window."
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-70 single-axis accelerometer window as recorded on cement or on carpet.
Who would use it
legged-robot developers doing online gait adaptation — Vail & Veloso frame it as choosing "a pregenerated set of parameters for movement on each particular surface"
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (walking surface under the robot (cement vs carpet)); label origin: source task.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
channel-selectionwindowingz-normalization
Leakage
fail documented or data-verified shared source
Split sources
Archive default split; who built it and by what rule is not documented R SR
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded three-axis accelerometer stream from a walking Sony AIBO ERS-210a quadruped robot; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. Two of the three accelerometer axes are discarded, so the vertical impact signature — arguably the most informative channel for substrate hardness — is not in the data. Each instance is a sub-second window cut from a five-minute continuous recording, so gait-cycle phase within a window is arbitrary. Z-normalization removes the absolute acceleration scale. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Dataset family
sony-aibo with SonyAIBORobotSurface2

both from the same Vail & Veloso robot recordings

Overall verdict
fails leakage
New case assumed
New recording occasions.
Split evidence
sub-second windows of one continuous five-minute recording per surface; the original authors used blocked cross-validation for this reason; the robot is deployed on a new day and floor patch R.

StarLightCurves SENSOR · Earth & space science · 1,000/8,236 train/test · length 1024 · 3 classes real tasksource task label Separatedassumed new case: new stars ordered, non-temporalphase (folded light curve) open question

Taskreal task

Archive task
variable-star class assigned by an expert (three classes)
Label attached by
Collectors source task SR
Verdict
real task

Variable-star classification is a standing task of survey astronomy (catalogue construction); the expert labels came with the data, while the linked papers used it for indexing and anomaly detection.

SplitSeparated

New case assumed
New stars
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The source-level record describes one separate star per series. On that description, the test contains stars absent from training, rather than another piece of a familiar star's recording. No shared-star concern is identified. Possible shared observing conditions or processing procedures were not separately checked.

Source

Automated classification of variable stars. Sky surveys produce brightness measurements of hundreds of millions of celestial objects, far more than astronomers can inspect; the standing task is to assign each variable star to its physical type from the shape of its light curve.

Publication
Keogh, Wei, Xi, Lee & Vlachos, "LB_Keogh Supports Exact Indexing of Shapes under Rotation Invariance with Arbitrary Representations and Distance Measures", VLDB 2006 (expanded version, read in full at cs.ucr.edu/~eamonn/VLDB2006_Expanded.pdf), Sec. 2.4 "Indexing Star Light Curves": "A star light curve is a time series of brightness of a celestial object as a function of time." And, decisively for the x-axis: "There are two things which make this difficult. First is the enormous volume of data, the second is the fact that while is it possible to extract a single period of a light curve, there is no natural starting point. In order the find the similarity of two light curves it is therefore necessary to compare every possible circular shift of the data [29], which as we show below corresponds exactly to the rotation invariance matching problem for shapes in the one-dimensional representation." The archive page confirms the folding: "This is a dataset with 1,000 (purportedly) phase-aligned starlight curves of length 1,024". The archive's other source link, Rebbapragada, Protopapas, Brodley & Alcock, "Finding Anomalous Periodic Time Series" (arXiv:0905.3428), was reached only at abstract level; it likewise stresses that "the periods of any given pair of light-curves may be out of sync".
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-1024 phase-folded stellar light curve into one of three expert-assigned variable-star classes.
Who would use it
survey astronomy — automated catalogue construction and anomaly triage for surveys producing more light curves than can be inspected by hand
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the expert-assigned variable-star class that came with the data from the Harvard Time Series Center (class names not documented in the linked sources); label origin: source task.

Data origin
ordered, non-temporal index: phase (folded light curve)

Index is phase: each series is a light curve folded on the star's period, so the ordered axis is phase within one cycle, not elapsed time.

Archive changes
resamplingz-normalization
Leakage
pass source separation by construction (each series is a distinct star)
Split sources
Archive default split; who built it and by what rule is not documented R R
Multichannel source
no
Dataset family
none from the same source task
Overall verdict
real task; split separated by construction
New case assumed
New stars.
Split evidence
each folded curve is one star; deployment classifies new stars, which a split over objects provides R. The index is phase of a folded light curve (period estimation done upstream and not released); filed as ordered, non-temporal R.
Open questions
Index is phase; a reader who regards a phase-folded curve as a time axis would count it as temporal.

The test set is the source study's own independent test set (370 of the 983 spectra held out). However, the value check finds one sequence present in both sets with different labels. Thus, the original split description does not resolve this inconsistency in the released data.

Why
This is a generated task rather than measurements from people, devices or specimens. The corresponding source-separation question is therefore not assessed here. Its split would instead need to be examined through the generation procedure; synthetic origin alone is not evidence of leakage.

Source

No real measurements exist. The six classes imitate the pattern vocabulary of statistical process control - the shapes a quality engineer is trained to spot on a manufacturing control chart (in control, cyclic, trending up/down, shifted up/down).

Publication
UCI KDD Archive dataset page (read), which names the generator - "Original Owner and Donor Dr Robert Alcock ... Date Donated - June 8, 1999. Data Characteristics - This dataset contains 600 examples of control charts synthetically generated by the process in Alcock and Manolopoulos (1999). ... The classes are organized as follows - 1-100 Normal, 101-200 Cyclic, 201-300 Increasing trend, 301-400 Decreasing trend, 401-500 Upward shift, 501-600 Downward shift". The primary conference paper (Alcock and Manolopoulos, 7th Hellenic Conference on Informatics, Ioannina 1999) was not reached, but its generating equations are reproduced in Geurts (2002) PhD thesis, App. B.2.4 "CC" (read - taking m = 30, s = 2 and r(t) uniform on [-3,3], Normal is m + s*r(t), Cyclic adds a*sin(2*pi*t/T) with a and T drawn in [10,15], Increasing adds g*t with g in [0.2,0.5], Upward adds a step x in [7.5,20] at a random time in [n/3, 2n/3], and the down-variants negate these).
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a univariate length-60 series into the six control-chart pattern classes.
Who would use it
none (synthetic demo). The class vocabulary is borrowed from control-chart pattern recognition in manufacturing quality control - whose real users would be process/quality engineers - but no process was ever measured; the UCI page cites Pham and Chan (1998) on control-chart pattern recognition as background.

fail toy source task

Why
The source contains repeated motion recordings from a small group of actors. We suspect that actors are shared between the sets, but the files lack actor identifiers for checking this. We therefore cannot establish performance on new people or new recording occasions, and should not present actor overlap as verified. Separately, the value check finds four test cases identical to training data.

Source

There is no original prediction problem. The series come from the CMU Graphics Lab Motion Capture Database, a general-purpose optical motion-capture resource built for graphics and animation research, in which each recorded take carries a free-text motion description.

Publication
CMU Graphics Lab Motion Capture Database (http://mocap.cs.cmu.edu/) as the data source, and Ye & Keogh, "Time series shapelets: a novel technique that allows accurate, interpretable and fast classification" (Data Mining and Knowledge Discovery, 2011) as the first use, per the archive page. The KDD 2009 conference version of the shapelet paper (cs.ucr.edu/~eamonn/shaplet.pdf) was read and does not describe this dataset; the DMKD 2011 journal version, which does, is paywalled and was not reached.

Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded optical motion capture of an actor walking; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. Motion capture gives a full 3D skeleton over time; this dataset keeps one coordinate of one marker, so the coordination between limbs - which is what actually distinguishes a limp from a normal gait - is gone. Truncation to a common length crops the ends of longer segments instead of resampling them, and z-normalisation removes stride amplitude. The source recording is multivariate, though the source posed no classification problem; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

Dataset family
toe-segmentation with ToeSegmentation2

the X and Y axes of the same body of CMU walking motions (case correspondence undocumented)

fails task construction and leakage

New people or recording occasions.

a small number of CMU actors with many takes each R; no subject id is released and the archive does not state whether takes of one actor were kept in one set, so actor sharing is inferred rather than observed; a gait screen is deployed on new people I.
Label
labels derived by the curators (Ye & Keogh) by keyword matching against CMU's free-text motion descriptions, as the archive page states S. The Concern verdict rests on the four exact cross-split matches D; actor sharing is inferred from the source design (few CMU actors, many takes) and not observed I.
Open questions
Concern rests on the exact cross-split matches alone; actor sharing is not established (ToeSegmentation2 has no match and is Unclear).

Why
Each series is described as a separate simulated event, so testing uses other generated events rather than another piece of one recording. No shared-event issue is identified by that description. Whether the generation process creates further dependencies would need its settings or code to check.

Source

Roverso's Transient Classification Benchmark (OECD Halden Reactor Project): simulated instrumentation-failure transients of a nuclear power plant, 16 fault classes with 50 four-feature instances each, built to compare transient classifiers; the archive uses a four-class, one-feature subset.

Publication
timeseriesclassification.com description page, which is the donor's own account: "This 4-class dataset is a subset of the Transient Classification Benchmark (trace project), an initiative at the turn of the century to collate data from the application domain of the process industry (e.g. nuclear, chemical, etc.). It is a synthetic dataset designed to simulate instrumentation failures in a nuclear power plant, created by Davide Roverso. The full dataset consists of 16 classes, 50 instances in each class. Each instance has 4 features. The TRACE subset only uses the second feature of class 2 and the third feature of class 3 and 7. Hence, this dataset contains 200 instances, 50 for each class. All instances are linearly interpolated to have the same length of 275 data points, and are z-normalized." Roverso's primary publication was not read: the archive's CiteSeerX link (doi 10.1.1.33.7261) now resolves only to a Wayback error page, and the corresponding journal article (Roverso, "Soft computing tools for transient classification", Information Sciences 127(3-4), 2000) is behind Elsevier's paywall (HTTP 403). The synthetic origin therefore rests on the donor-written archive page alone.
Full record

Who would use it
none directly (simulated benchmark data); the motivating stakeholder is nuclear/process-plant operations, where transient identification supports operator decision aids

Why

Source

Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded multi-lead long-term ambulatory ECG of one 58-year-old male; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. Each beat is presented as an isolated univariate series, so the pairing between the two leads of the same beat - the information a cardiologist actually uses when reading a multi-lead ECG - is thrown away. Z-normalisation removes absolute amplitude, which is one of the main things that differs between leads; what remains is lead-specific waveform morphology (R/S ratio, T-wave polarity). The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive; no multivariate version of it is in UEA.

lead label assigned by the curator (E. Keogh) over a PhysioNet recording S. The class is the ECG lead (signal 0 vs 1) of one ten-minute window of one PhysioNet long-term recording (ltdb 15814) S; no paper posing this task was found R.

TwoPatterns SIMULATED · Simulated · 1,000/4,000 train/test · length 128 · 4 classes failstoy source task Generatedassumed new case: new generated examples syntheticgenerator step

Source task
toy task None; designed for thesis experiments

Why
This is a generated task rather than measurements from people, devices or specimens. The corresponding source-separation question is therefore not assessed here. Its split would instead need to be examined through the generation procedure; synthetic origin alone is not evidence of leakage.

Source

No real-world problem exists. Pierre Geurts designed the "Two-pat" problem for the time-series part of his PhD thesis, to test whether a classifier can detect two local patterns and, crucially, their order.

Publication
Geurts, "Contributions to decision tree induction - bias/variance tradeoff and time series classification", PhD thesis, University of Liege, 2002, App. B.2.3 "Two-pat" (read, p. 237) - "This dataset has also be designed by us for our experiments. In this problem, each scenario is described by a single temporal input attribute A which is defined on the integer time axis 0,1,...,127"; the patterns are "s1 and s2 defining the patterns of respective lengths l1 and l2", "e(t) is some noise surrounding the patterns, drawn from a standard normal distribution N(0,1)", "l1 and l2 are integers uniformly drawn in [16;32]" and t1, t2 are drawn in [0;127] so that the two patterns do not overlap; the up/down steps take values -5 then +5 (us) or +5 then -5 (ds), each chosen with probability 0.5, and Eq. B.4 defines the four classes uu, ud, du, dd, which "are thus equiprobable".
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a univariate length-128 series into the four ordered step-pair classes.
Who would use it
none (synthetic demo)
Task construction
fail toy source task

Benchmark target: Ordered pair of up/down steps in noise. Source target or purpose: None; designed for thesis experiments. Label attached by: Generator. Source

No real-world problem exists. UMD is a synthetic three-class benchmark from the same donor as BME: a small bell appears near the start or the end of the series and the class is whether that bell points up (Up), is absent (Middle), or points down (Down).

Publication
No publication found. The only provenance is the donor line on the archive page: "Data source: University Joseph Fourier (Grenoble I), Laboratoire d'informatique de Grenoble(LIG), Equipe AMA" (timeseriesclassification.com, UMD), with no data_source_link. As for BME, the likely origin is the Grenoble AMA group (candidate: Frambourg, Douzal-Chouakria & Gaussier, IDA 2013), but that text could not be reached (HAL access denied, ResearchGate 403) and no consulted source states UMD was introduced there.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a univariate length-150 series as Up, Middle or Down.
Who would use it
none (synthetic demo)
Task construction
fail toy source task

Benchmark target: Bell pointing up, absent, or pointing down. Source target or purpose: None; unpublished generator (Grenoble LIG/AMA). Label attached by: Generator. Mechanism 'toy source task': the data were simulated or staged by method developers and the classes chosen to demonstrate a method, not to answer an external question.

Data origin
synthetic index: generator step
Archive changes
simulation

n/a (generated) not assessed (generated data)

Why
The original system recognises gestures for familiar users, so sharing users can be appropriate. The missing information is whether all recordings from a day were kept together. Without this, the test could contain gestures recorded on a day already represented in training, rather than gestures collected when the user returns.

Source

Liu, Zhong, Wickramasuriya and Vasudevan built uWave, a personalized gesture-recognition system driven by a single three-axis accelerometer, so that consumer devices could be controlled by hand gestures with only one training sample per gesture.

Publication
Liu, Zhong, Wickramasuriya, Vasudevan, "uWave: Accelerometer-based Personalized Gesture Recognition and Its Applications", PerCom 2009 / Pervasive and Mobile Computing 5(6), 2009. Read in full (author copy, yecl.org/publications/liu09percom.pdf).
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-945 series - the x, y and z accelerometer channels concatenated - as one of the eight uWave vocabulary gestures.
Who would use it
designers of gesture-based interaction for consumer electronics and mobile devices; also proposed by the authors for gesture-based user authentication
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (which of the eight predefined vocabulary gestures was performed); label origin: source task.

Data origin
ordered, non-temporal index: time (three axes concatenated)

Three accelerometer axes concatenated end to end; within each block the index is time, across blocks it is not, so the series as a whole is ordered but not temporal.

Archive changes
concatenationz-normalization
Leakage
unknown source separation undocumented
Split sources
Archive split 896 train / 3582 test over the whole uWave library; not documented as user- or day-disjoint; no ids ship R R
Multichannel source
no
Dataset family
uwave with UWaveGestureLibraryX, UWaveGestureLibraryY, UWaveGestureLibraryZ, UWaveGestureLibraryUEA

X, Y and Z are the three accelerometer axes of the same gestures, All concatenates them; UEA UWaveGestureLibrary is a multivariate subsample of the same library

Overall verdict
real task; leakage unknown
New case assumed
New days or recording occasions.
Split evidence
uWave is personalised (one training sample per gesture per user), so the right split is by day within user; the archive's 896/3582 split over the whole library is not documented as day-disjoint and no ids ship R. The x, y and z accelerometer channels are concatenated end to end; within each block the index is time R.

yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive, where it already exists as UWaveGestureLibrary.

Wafer SENSOR · Industrial, sensors & robotics · 1,000/6,164 train/test · length 152 · 2 classes real tasksource task label Concernshared source temporaltime single channel

Taskreal task

Source task
real task whether the process run recorded by the sensor was normal or abnormal, as judged by a domain expert
Archive task
whether the process run recorded by the sensor was normal or abnormal, as judged by a domain expert
Label attached by
Source study source task S
Verdict
real task

The source study posed a classification target that a stakeholder outside the paper needed.

SplitConcern

New case assumed
New wafers or production runs
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The archive ships all six sensor traces of each wafer as separate cases and split them without regard to wafer or run. Matching every shipped series to the raw files shows 690 wafers and 99 of 104 production runs on both sides; every training series has another sensor of its wafer in test. The test therefore does not measure performance on new wafers or runs.

Source

Semiconductor manufacturing process control. Plasma-etch tools used to fabricate silicon wafers carry large numbers of in-line sensors; if a tool drifts outside tolerance during any of the 250-plus processing steps, yield and reliability suffer or the wafer is scrapped.

Publication
Olszewski, "Generalized Feature Extraction for Structural Pattern Recognition in Time-Series Data", PhD thesis, Carnegie Mellon University, CMU-CS-01-108, 2001 (read in full from cs.cmu.edu/~bobski). Sec. 4.3.2, p. 65: "A collection of in-line process-control measurements recorded from various sensors during the processing of silicon wafers for semiconductor fabrication constitute the wafer database; each data set in the wafer database contains the measurements recorded by one sensor during the processing of one wafer by one tool." Same section, p. 66: "The data sets contained in each database were analyzed by appropriate domain experts, and a label of normal or abnormal was assigned to each data set. Of the 1194 data sets in the wafer database, 1067 data sets were identified as normal and 127 data sets were identified as abnormal." On which sensors: p. 66-67, "six parameters have been identified by domain experts as being critical for monitoring purposes: radio frequency forward power, radio frequency reflected power, chamber pressure, 405 nanometer (nm) emission, 520 nanometer (nm) emission, and direct current bias" and p. 69, "Previous experiments have shown that the most accurate classification of wafer data sets can be accomplished using the 405 nm and 520 nm parameters."
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-152 etch-tool sensor trace as a normal or abnormal processing run.
Who would use it
semiconductor fabs running statistical process control on etch tools — catching an out-of-tolerance run early avoids scrapping wafers
Task construction
pass none (source task label)

The benchmark label is the target the source study posed (whether the process run recorded by the sensor was normal or abnormal, as judged by a domain expert); label origin: source task.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
resamplingz-normalization
Leakage
fail documented or data-verified shared source
Split sources
Archive default split, 1000 train / 6164 test; not stated in Olszewski's thesis, author and rule undocumented R R
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

Each wafer was recorded by six in-line sensors. The release ships all six traces as separate univariate series and drops the sensor identity and the wafer and run grouping, so the multivariate structure a process-control system would use is gone. The thesis itself classified single sensors (the 405 nm and 520 nm parameters) and left combining them to future work; no multivariate version is in UEA.

Dataset family
none from the same source task
Overall verdict
fails leakage
New case assumed
New wafers or production runs.
Split evidence
the archive pools all six sensors of each wafer, and 690 wafers and 99 of 104 runs have series on both sides, measured by matching every shipped series to the raw files D. The reconstruction reproduces the shipped values but is not the historical conversion code R.

Permalink · CSV row: Wafer

Source

Varietal authentication of red wine: the archive says the two classes are Cabernet Sauvignon and Shiraz and that the spectra were obtained by FTIR with attenuated total reflectance sampling, which places it in the same Institute of Food Research (Kemsley, Norwich) food-authentication family as Coffee, Meat, OliveOil and Strawbe…

Publication
Not found. The archive page gives no citation, only "donated by K. Kemsley, A. Bagnall" and a data_source_link to the IFR "Example Datasets for Download" page. Three Internet Archive snapshots of that page (2013, 2014, 2016) were retrieved: it lists strawberry purees, minced meats, olive oils, dry-cured ham SDS-PAGE lanes, coffee, DGGE lanes and facial EMG - but no wine dataset. A Europe PMC author search for Kemsley EK (58 records) returns no paper on wine. Bagnall et al. (SDM 2012) Sec. 3.1, which introduced the other IFR spectrograms to the TSC literature, covers only beef, coffee and olive oil. So the only evidence for the account above is the archive page itself.

Source target or purpose: word spotting, matching word images by their ASCII tags to index the George Washington manuscripts (Rath & Manmatha 2003, Sec. 3.1-3.2). Label attached by: Curators. Mechanism 'task replacement': the archive replaces the source's label with a different task, or defines classes by its own rule where the source posed none; here the 50 words chosen for FiftyWords (not a class set of the source) were remapped to 25 groups on an undocumented criterion (archive page: 'remapped to 25 classes').

Worms MOTION · Biology & ecology · 181/77 train/test · length 900 · 5 classes failsmetadata as label Concernduplicates 13% temporaltime single channelfamily: wormsopen question

Taskfails

Source task
none Unsupervised discovery of behavioural motifs across strains (Brown et al. 2013); no classification target
Archive task
worm strain: N2 wild type or one of the mutants goa-1, unc-1, unc-38, unc-63
Label attached by
Curators metadata S
Verdict
fails metadata as label

The source collected the data but posed no classification target: Brown et al. built an unbiased dictionary of behavioural motifs, and the strain of each recorded worm was the experimental factor, not a prediction target.

SplitConcern

New case assumed
New worms or recordings
Exact duplicates
10 of 77 test series (13%) exactly match a training series D
Why
The original record describes each case as a separate worm recording. However, the value check finds 10 of 77 test cases identical to training cases. We therefore need worm and recording identifiers to resolve the disagreement between that source description and the released values.

Source

Behavioural genetics in the nematode Caenorhabditis elegans. Worm locomotion is a standard readout for gene function: mutants move differently from wild type, so quantifying movement is a way to phenotype genes.

Publication
Brown AE, Yemini EI, Grundy LJ, Jucikas T, Schafer WR, "A dictionary of behavioral motifs reveals clusters of genes affecting Caenorhabditis elegans locomotion", PNAS 110(2):791-796, 2013. Only the abstract was reached (the PNAS full text returned HTTP 403 and PubMed/EuropePMC did not render); the quoted sentence above is from that abstract. The C. elegans behavioural database (http://wormbehavior.mrc-lmb.cam.ac.uk/) is named by the archive page as the source of the traces, and the archive page reproduces the formatting description from Bagnall et al.'s bake-off paper.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-900 first-eigenworm amplitude series (one worm, ~15 minutes of crawling at 1 Hz) into one of five strains. 181 train / 77 test.
Who would use it
Geneticists and neurobiologists using C. elegans as a model organism, who need high-throughput behavioural phenotyping to link genes to behaviour.
Task construction
fail metadata as label

Benchmark target: worm strain: N2 wild type or one of the mutants goa-1, unc-1, unc-38, unc-63. Source target or purpose: Unsupervised discovery of behavioural motifs across strains (Brown et al. 2013); no classification target. Label attached by: Curators. Mechanism 'metadata as label': the archive derives the classes from a recording attribute; the strain is known before recording and was the source's experimental factor.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
channel-selectionresamplingz-normalization
Leakage
fail exact cross-split matches (10 of 77 test series)
Split sources
181 train / 77 test, identical for Worms and WormsTwoClass; rule undocumented R DR
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded video of a single worm crawling on an agar plate, tracked at 30 frames per second; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. Two reductions, both large. (1) Shape: each video frame's full worm outline is collapsed to a single scalar, the amplitude along the first eigenworm - three of the four shape dimensions are discarded, so postures that differ in eigenworms 2-4 are indistinguishable here. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive, where it already exists as EigenWorms.

Dataset family
worms with WormsTwoClass, EigenWormsUEA

Worms and WormsTwoClass are the same traces with the same split, mutant strains collapsed in the latter; UEA EigenWorms derives from the same C. elegans behavioural database

Overall verdict
fails task construction and leakage
New case assumed
New worms or recordings.
Split evidence
each series is one worm's recording; plate or batch is not shipped R. Strain labels used as classes where the source study was unsupervised motif discovery over the same strains; the strain is recording metadata, so the task is counted as constructed S.
Open questions
The strains were the source's experimental factor, so a strain classifier is a supervised proxy for the phenotyping question the study asked; we count the label as metadata because the source posed no classification target.

Permalink · CSV row: Worms

Brown AE, Yemini EI, Grundy LJ, Jucikas T, Schafer WR, "A dictionary of behavioral motifs reveals clusters of genes affecting Caenorhabditis elegans locomotion", PNAS 110(2):791-796, 2013. Only the abstract was reached (the PNAS full text returned HTTP 403 and PubMed/EuropePMC did not render); the quoted sentence above is from that abstract. The C. elegans behavioural database (http://wormbehavior.mrc-lmb.cam.ac.uk/) is named by the archive page as the source of the traces, and the archive page reproduces the formatting description from Bagnall et al.'s bake-off paper.
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-900 first-eigenworm amplitude series (one worm, ~15 minutes of crawling at 1 Hz) as wild-type (N2) or mutant. 181 train / 77 test.
Who would use it
Geneticists and neurobiologists using C. elegans as a model organism, who need high-throughput behavioural phenotyping to link genes to behaviour.
Task construction
fail metadata as label

Benchmark target: wild type vs mutant (the four mutant strains collapsed into one class). Source target or purpose: Unsupervised discovery of behavioural motifs across strains (Brown et al. 2013); no classification target. Label attached by: Curators. Mechanism 'metadata as label': the archive derives the classes from a recording attribute; the strain is known before recording and was the source's experimental factor.

Data origin
temporal index: time
Archive changes
channel-selectionresamplingz-normalization
Leakage
fail exact cross-split matches (10 of 77 test series)
Split sources
181 train / 77 test, identical for Worms and WormsTwoClass; rule undocumented R DR
Multichannel source
yes, one channel from a multichannel recording

The source recorded video of a single worm crawling on an agar plate, tracked at 30 frames per second; the UCR release keeps a single channel of it. Two reductions, both large. (1) Shape: each video frame's full worm outline is collapsed to a single scalar, the amplitude along the first eigenworm - three of the four shape dimensions are discarded, so postures that differ in eigenworms 2-4 are indistinguishable here. The problem the source posed is multivariate, and its place is the multivariate archive, where it already exists as EigenWorms.

Dataset family
worms with Worms, EigenWormsUEA

Worms and WormsTwoClass are the same traces with the same split, mutant strains collapsed in the latter; UEA EigenWorms derives from the same C. elegans behavioural database

Overall verdict
fails task construction and leakage
New case assumed
New worms or acquisitions.
Split evidence
each series is one worm's recording; plate or batch is not shipped R. Strain labels used as classes where the source study was unsupervised motif discovery over the same strains; the strain is recording metadata, so the task is counted as constructed S; the four mutant strains are collapsed into one class S.
Open questions
The strains were the source's experimental factor, so a strain classifier is a supervised proxy for the phenotyping question the study asked; we count the label as metadata because the source posed no classification target. Four mutant strains collapsed into one class.

Verdict
fails instance identity as label · toy source task (primary: instance identity as label)

The source data were staged or generated to demonstrate a method.

SplitConcern

New case assumed
New filming occasions
Exact duplicates
none found D
Why
The task identifies two actors from video frames. Measured on the shipped files, train and test come from one pool of frames: no test series is identical to a training series, but every training frame has a near-twin in test (median 2.09 vs 23.8 for random pairs), and the training set is indistinguishable from a random draw of test frames. The test does not establish identification in footage collected on another occasion.

Source

Built by Wei & Keogh (KDD 2006, Sec. 4.5) as a "realistic dataset" for their semi-supervised time-series classification method: "The dataset was obtained by capturing two actors transiting between yoga poses in front of a green screen." The task is to tell the two actors apart - "In total, we have 316 time series with an averag…

Publication
Wei & Keogh, "Semi-Supervised Time Series Classification", KDD 2006, Sec. 4.5 (read).
Full record
Benchmark task
Classify a length-426 body-outline series as the female or the male actor.
Who would use it
none applied; the data were captured to demonstrate a semi-supervised learning method, so the "problem" is already benchmark-shaped
Task construction
fail instance identity as label · toy source task (primary: instance identity as label)

Benchmark target: Which of two actors, from silhouettes. Source target or purpose: Actor discrimination, posed by the collectors as a demo task. Label attached by: Collectors (= curators). Mechanism 'instance identity as label': the archive uses the recording's source (subject, sensor or actor) as the class. Mechanism 'toy source task': the data were simulated or staged by method developers and the classes chosen to demonstrate a method, not to answer an external question. Listed under two mechanisms; counted once, under 'instance identity as label'.

no
Dataset family

fails task construction and leakage
New case assumed
New filming occasions.
Split evidence
measured on the shipped files, every training frame has a near-twin in test and the training set is indistinguishable from a random draw of test frames D; that the split therefore shares filming occasions is inferred from these distances, literal frame adjacency being unrecoverable R.
Label
Wei & Keogh captured the two actors themselves and posed the actor-discrimination task in KDD 2006 (collectors = curators); the series is a body outline (index = contour arc-length) S.
Open questions
Collectors posed the actor task themselves (collectors = curators); listed under both instance identity and toy source.

Built from ucr_provenance_audit.csv on 2026-11-06. Choices within one filter group combine with OR; filter groups combine with AND. The URL saves your selection; #open=PigAirwayPressure links to a record.

The authors set the rules. Agents drafted and checked the records. Two agents independently re-derived every verdict; a third reconciled them. The authors decided every uncertain verdict and re-read sources for contested cases. Found an error? Open an issue. No analytics, no cookies.
